Rigidity aspects of singular patches in stratified flows
Taoufik Hmidi, Haroune Houamed, Mohamed Zerguine

Abstract
. We explore the local well-posedness theory for the 2d inviscid Boussinesq system when the vorticity is given by a singular patch. We give a significant improvement of [ 39 ] by replacing their compatibility assumption on the density with a constraint on its platitude degree on the singular set. The second main contribution focuses on the same issue for the partial viscous Boussinesq system. We establish a uniform LWP theory with respect to the vanishing conductivity. This issue is much more delicate than the inviscid case and one should carefully deal with various difficulties related to the diffusion effects which tend to alter some local structures. The weak a priori estimates are not trivial and refined analysis on transport-diffusion equation subject to a logarithmic singular potential is required. Another difficulty stems from some commutators arising in the control of the co-normal regularity that we counterbalance in part by the maximal smoothing effects of transport-diffusion equation advected by a velocity field which scales slightly below the Lipschitz class.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q35; 35Q31.

1.
Introduction

The atmosphere motion and the ocean circulation is an area of intensive activity offering challenging problems to explore. The governing equations stem from the thermodynamics and mechanics laws, and can be expressed in general as a system of nonlinear partial differential equations with higher degree of complexity. To capture the prominent feature of large scale motion, some approximations around equilibrium states are used in order to simplify slightly the equations. Notice that the aforementioned equilibrium states are generally obtained from the hydrostatic or the geostrophic balances driven by the stratification, the gravitation and earth rotation mechanisms. There are several models used in the literature and in what follows we shall be concerned with the Boussinesq system. In this case, the fluid motion is mainly influenced by the velocity field and the density with the assumption that in the momentum equation the density effect is only sensitive for the gravitational force, see for instance
[
63
]
. The outcome of this analysis in the plane is the following full viscous Boussinesq system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v-\mu\Delta v+\nabla p=\rho\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\quad(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2},&\\
\partial_{t}\rho+v\cdot\nabla\rho-\kappa\Delta\rho=0,&\\
\textnormal{div }v=0,&\\
(v,\rho)|_{t=0}=(v_{0},\rho_{0}),\end{array}\right.

(B
μ,κ
)

where
v
is the velocity field in the plane,
\rho
is a scalar function that stands for the fluid density, and
p
is the pressure which is a scalar potential that can be connected to the velocity and the density through the incompressibility assumption
\textnormal{div }v=0.
The parameters
\mu,\kappa
are positive real numbers called the viscosity and the conductivity, respectively.
For fluids with constant density, the system (
Bμ,κ
) boils down to the classical Navier-Stokes equations,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v-\mu\Delta v+\nabla p=0,&\\
\textnormal{div }v=0,&\\
v_{|t=0}={v}_{0},\end{array}\right.

(NS
μ
)

which in turn reduces to the incompressible Euler equation (E) in the absence of the viscosity.
It is convenient in the planar case to take advantage of the vorticity structure and write down the equations in terms of vorticity and density. Actually, the vorticity associated to the velocity field is determined by the scalar function

\omega\triangleq\partial_{1}v^{2}-\partial_{2}v^{1}=\nabla^{\perp}\cdot v,\quad\nabla^{\perp}=(-\partial_{2},\partial_{1}).

Then the system (
Bμ,κ
) can be transformed into the equivalent reformulation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\omega+v\cdot\nabla\omega-\mu\Delta\omega=\partial_{1}\rho,&\\
\partial_{t}\rho+v\cdot\nabla\rho-\kappa\Delta\rho=0,&\\
v=\Delta^{-1}\nabla^{\perp}\omega,&\\
(\omega,\rho)|_{t=0}=(\omega_{0},\rho_{0}).\end{array}\right.

(VD
μ,κ
)

Now, the main task is to overview the status of the art with respect to the well-posedness theory for these models. There are a lot of important results achieved during the past decades and we shall restrict the discussion to some of them.

➊
Global well-posedness: from Euler to N-S equations
. The construction of global unique solutions in the Hölder class
C^{k,\alpha}
with
k\geq 1
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
was performed many years ago in the prominent works of Hölder
[
48
]
and Wölibner
[
70
]
. Later, similar results were obtained by Kato
[
50
]
in Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, with
s>2.
The main ingredients for getting global solutions are the conservation of the
L^{\infty}
norm of the vorticity which is simply advected by the flow and a logarithmic type estimate. For more results and expository texts we refer to
[
9
,
10
,
14
,
18
,
32
,
51
,
55
,
62
,
69
]
. By taking advantage of the vorticity advection, Yudovich succeeded in
[
73
]
to relax the classical theory and generate global unique solutions when the initial vorticity is bounded and integrable. In this setting the velocity field is not necessary Lipschitz but belongs to the class of log-Lipschitz functions. As a by-product, we obtain the global persistence and uniqueness with the vortex patch structure. More precisely, if the initial vorticity
\omega_{0}={\bf 1}_{\Omega_{0}}
is a patch, that is, the characteristic function of a bounded domain then the transported vorticity being also a patch
\omega(t)={\bf 1}_{\Omega_{t}}
such that
\Omega_{t}\triangleq\Psi(t,\Omega_{0})
, where
\Psi
is the flow associated to the velocity field. In this specific case we reduce the dynamics to the boundary motion in the plane, which is subject to an integro-differential equation. The global in time persistence of the boundary regularity was accomplished in a remarkable work of Chemin
[
17
]
for a large class of data covering in particular simply connected patches with
C^{1+\varepsilon}-
boundary. The proof was slightly simplified for the patches by Bertozzi and Constantin in
[
7
]
. We point out that Chemin’s toolbox is robust and flexible and it allows him to tackle through suitable adaptations the case of singular patches. He obtained under suitable assumptions the regularity persistence of the smooth part of the boundary. For more discussion and extension on this subject we may refer to the papers
[
3
,
7
,
8
,
22
,
23
,
30
,
31
,
35
,
65
,
66
,
72
,
49
,
60
]
and the references therein.

As regards to the Navier-Stokes system (
NSμ
), the local/global well-posedness theory in any dimension space is one of the most studied subject in fluid dynamics and we may find a complete review on the recent developments on this topic in the textbooks
[
53
,
64
]
. Here, we shall only go over some results that fit with our main scope related to the smooth and singular patch problem. The first result was achieved by Danchin
[
24
]
, who asserts that if
\omega_{0}={\bf 1}_{\Omega_{0}}
with
\partial\Omega_{0}
is a Jordan curve with
C^{1+\varepsilon}
regularity then the velocity field of (
NSμ
) remains Lipschitz for any time uniformly with respect to the viscosity. In addition, the image of the initial boundary by the viscous flow is
C^{1+\varepsilon^{\prime}}
for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
. This regularity loss is attributed to the use of Besov spaces
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
with
p\in[1,\infty)
where some maximal smoothing effects of transport-diffusion equations are established using energy estimates. Unfortunately, with this techniques, Hölder spaces seem to be out of reach due to the amplification of the estimates when
p
grows to
\infty
. To remedy to this defect and meanwhile show the regularity persistence, the first author extended in
[
40
]
the smoothing effects to this borderline case performing a new method based on Lagrangian coordinates and a frequency double localization lemma established by Vishik in
[
69
]
. Notice that in both papers, and compared to the inviscid case, the regularity persistence is a delicate issue where one should carefully deal with two opposite facets of the viscosity. The worst one manifests when we want to track the co-normal regularity of the vorticity and in this case new commutators between suitable vector-fields and the Laplacian operator emerge, generating a loss of regularity. To counterbalance this side effect and repair the damage caused by the Laplacian, we use its maximal smoothing effects. As to the singular viscous patches, the literature is slightly poor and we refer to the result of the first author
[
40
]
. It was shown that starting with
\omega_{0}={\bf 1}_{\Omega_{0}}
where the boundary
\partial\Omega_{0}
is assumed to be
C^{1+\varepsilon}
outside a singular set
\Sigma_{0}
, we find that the velocity field is Lipschitz away the transported singular set by the flow, uniformly in the vanishing viscosity. It was also proved that the image by the viscous flow of the smooth part is in
C^{1+\varepsilon^{\prime}}
for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
. It is worthy to note that the velocity scales slightly below the Lipschitz class leading to serious difficult technical problems. For instance, we cannot reach the maximal smoothing effect in transport-diffusion equation and logarithmic loss of regularity sounds to be unavoidable. This is the main technical factor behind the loss of regularity of the boundary and we will come back later with more explanations when we shall comment our main results and explore the proofs.

➋
Well-posedness for partial viscous Boussinesq system.
There is a lot of activities around this subject depending on whether the viscosities are vanishing or not. In the full viscous case
\mu,\kappa>0
, global well-posed results were established in
[
12
,
37
]
using the energy estimates method. For the partial viscous case
\mu>0,\kappa=0,
global well-posedness was analyzed by Hou and Li
[
47
]
and Chae
[
13
]
in the framework of Sobolev spaces
H^{s},s>2
. Later, Abidi and Hmidi
[
2
]
succeeded to improve the previous results for
(v_{0},\rho_{0})\in B^{-1}_{\infty,1}\cap L^{2}\times B^{0}_{2,1}
. The case
v^{0},\rho_{0}\in L^{2}
was solved by Danchin and Paicu in
[
26
]
. In the partial viscous case
\mu=0,\kappa>0
, similar studies have been carried out through various papers and we shall only cite some of them. In
[
13
]
, Chae proved the global well-posedness for
v_{0},\rho_{0}\in H^{s}
for
s>2
which was improved by Hmidi and Keraani in
[
42
]
for the critical Besov spaces, that is
(v_{0},\rho_{0})\in B^{1+2/p}_{p,1}\times B^{-1+{2}/{p}}_{p,1}\cap L^{r},\;r>2
. In the Yudovich setting, Danchin and Paicu
[
25
]
obtained the global existence and uniqueness when
(v^{0},\rho^{0})\in L^{2}\times L^{2}\cap B_{\infty,1}^{-1}
and
\omega^{0}\in L^{r}\cap L^{\infty}
with
r\geqslant 2
. We emphasize that a lot of important developments regarding closely connected models have been conducted over the past few decades. For more details, we refer to the papers
[
6
,
15
,
16
,
36
,
45
,
52
,
54
,
58
,
67
,
68
,
71
]
and the references therein.

➌
Inviscid Boussinesq system
. It corresponds to the system (
Bμ,κ
) with
\mu=\kappa=0
, and it will simply be denoted by (
B0
). Its vorticity-density reformulation reads as follows

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\omega+v\cdot\nabla\omega=\partial_{1}\rho,&\\
\partial_{t}\rho+v\cdot\nabla\rho=0,\,v=\Delta^{-1}\nabla^{\perp}\omega\\
(\omega,\rho)|_{t=0}=(\omega_{0},\rho_{0}).\end{array}\right.

(B
0
)

Local well-posedness theory was established in the setting of sub-critical Sobolev spaces
v_{0},\rho_{0}\in H^{s},s>2
or Hölder spaces
v_{0},\rho_{0}\in C^{k+\epsilon},k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star},\varepsilon\in(0,1)
by Chae and Nam in
[
15
,
16
]
. Furthermore, they formulated a blow-up criterion related only to the density in the following sense: if
T^{\star}
is the maximal lifespan of the solution of (
B0
) then

T^{\star}<\infty\Longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T^{\star}}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}d\tau=\infty.

This criterion remains valid for (
Bμ,κ
) for different values of
\mu,\kappa
. The blow-up of classics solutions has been addressed very recently in two relevant papers
[
33
,
19
]
. In the first one, Elgindi proved that the blow-up in finite time occurs for smooth solutions when the fluid is evolving in a singular domain, typically an infinite sector. However, in
[
19
]
Chen and Hou were able to show the finite time singularity formation in a half-plane domain for slightly rough velocity belonging to the Hölder class
C^{1+\epsilon}
, with
\epsilon
sufficiently small.

Concerning the construction of Yudovich type solutions, the situation turns out to be more intricate and highly critical compared to Euler equations. Actually, from the first equation of (
B0
) we infer

\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}d\tau.

Then, differentiating the second equation of (
B0
)

(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)\partial_{j}\rho=\partial_{j}v\cdot\nabla\rho.

Consequently, the estimate of
\|\nabla\rho(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}
requires the source term to be bounded, which is not the case in general because the velocity field is not Lipschitz in the framework of Yudovich solutions. This suggests that all the scenarios of well/ill-posedness are likely possible for this model, a fact that was confirmed by some recent works. Indeed, on one hand, according to Elgindi and Masmoudi contribution
[
32
]
an ill-posed result connected to some instability estimates in the Yudovich setting can be achieved with special initial data. On the second hand, a well-posedness result in the smooth/singular patch class was obtained by Hassainia and Hmidi
[
39
]
. Although the patch structure is destroyed during the dynamics, one can still take advantage of the flexible vortex patch formalism developed by Chemin
[
17
]
and prove that in the smooth case, the velocity remains Lipschitz for a short time. However, when the patch is singular, a compatibility condition was required in
[
39
]
stating that the density should be constant around the singular set of the boundary. This property is conserved along the dynamics due to the transport equation, and this was a key observation in their proof. It was left open the question of whether local well-posedness result can be performed for the partial viscous case (B
0,κ
) uniformly on the vanishing conductivity when the initial patch is singular. The presence of the dissipation induces a smoothing effect which unfortunately alters the compatibility condition affecting seriously the main ingredients in their arguments. In the smooth case, it turns out in view of the recent work of Meddour
[
56
]
, that a positive answer for the local well-posedness problem can be obtained using the same lines of the proof. More results and extensions to different models can be found in
[
27
,
28
,
34
,
46
,
61
,
74
]
.

✦
Aims and main results.

The main task of the current work is to see whether we can relax the compatibility assumption needed in
[
39
]
and meanwhile obtain a uniform result for the partial viscous system (B
0,κ
). This system will be denoted in short (
Bκ
) and takes in the vorticity-density formulation the form

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\omega+v\cdot\nabla\omega=\partial_{1}\rho,&\\
\partial_{t}\rho+v\cdot\nabla\rho-\kappa\Delta\rho=0,\\
v=\Delta^{-1}\nabla^{\perp}\omega,&\\
(\omega,\rho)|_{t=0}=(\omega_{0},\rho_{0}).\end{array}\right.

(B
κ
)

As we have mentioned before, singular patches framework offers a delicate subject to analyze with regard to the local well-posedness problem for the system (
B0
). In
[
39
]
, this was achieved only when the initial density is constant around the singular part of the domain. By doing that we suppress the singularity effects during the motion and prevent them to interact with the density to avoid any possibility for the norm inflation. It is likely possible to conceive a well-prepared configuration where the singularity of the patch could breakdown the local well posedness through some sort of instabilities. This is a relevant and important issue which sounds to be out of reach of the techniques used in the vortex patch problem. Here we fix another connected target aiming to cover more class of initial data on the density, still with singular patches, leading to a well-posedness result.

Our first contribution concerns the inviscid system (
B0
) and we shall extend the result of
[
39
]
. More precisely, we will prove the following theorem whose general statement is detailed in Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
and
\Omega_{0}
be a bounded domain of the plane whose boundary
\partial\Omega_{0}
is a Jordan curve of class
C^{1+s}
outside a closed countable set
\Sigma_{0}\subset\partial\Omega_{0}
.
Let
\omega_{0}=1_{\Omega_{0}}
and
\rho_{0}\in C^{1+s}\cap W^{1,a}
, for some
a\in(1,\infty)
.
Suppose that
\nabla\rho_{0}
is identically zero on
\Sigma_{0}
.
Then, there exists
T>0
such that the system (
B0
) admits a unique local solution
(\omega,\rho)
satisfying

\omega,\rho,\nabla\rho\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T],L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}).

Furthermore, the velocity field
v
satisfies

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}\frac{\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{h}^{c})}}{-\log h}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]),

where
\Sigma_{t}\triangleq\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0})
and

(\Sigma_{t})_{h}^{c}\triangleq\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2};\,d\big(x,\Sigma_{t}\big)\geqslant h\big\}.

In addition, the boundary of
\Psi(t,\Omega_{0})
is locally
C^{1+s}
outside the set
\Sigma_{t}
.

We first remark that our theorem improves considerably the result of
[
39
]
where it was assumed that
\nabla\rho_{0}
vanishes in a small neighborhood of the singular set
\Sigma_{0}
. In our result, we need just this function to vanish exactly at the singular set. Let us now turn to the proof, sketch the main ideas and provide some details on the major difficulties that we have encountered and how to solve them. Below we shall explore the different steps implemented during the proof.

\x⃝

L^{p}
-estimates.
In this step, we establish weak a priori estimates for
\omega
and
\nabla\rho
in
L^{p}
with
p\in[a,\infty]
. The full statement is described in Proposition
3.1
where the regularity and the vanishing condition imposed to
\nabla\rho
are simply replaced by a slightly weaker assumption connected to the platitude degree of
\nabla\rho_{0}
near the singular set as stated in (
3.6
). From this proposition, we succeeded to follow the time evolution of the platitude degree which is decaying and remains strictly positive during a short time. This fact is crucial to derive the required a priori estimates at the
L^{p}
level following an energy method. Let us give a quick insight into how one could follow the platitude degree of
\nabla\rho(t)
. For this aim we simply proceed intuitively with a weighted transport localization around the singular set
\Sigma_{t}\triangleq\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0})
combined with energy estimates where the transport structure plays a central role to close the estimates. We use in a particular way a fundamental fact ensuring that the velocity is Lipschitz when we cut at some distance
h
from the singular set and the maximum growth of the gradient behaves like
-\log h.

\x⃝

Co-normal regularity.
In this part we perform suitable tangential regularity estimates of the vorticity
\partial_{X}\omega
using suitable transported vector-fields capturing the main feature of the boundary regularity. We follow basically the same arguments developed in
[
39
]
. The main estimates related to this discussion will be explored in Proposition
3.2
and Proposition
3.3
.

\x⃝
Existence and uniqueness .
The construction of the solutions and the checking of the uniqueness part will be analyzed along Section
3.3
. It will be conducted in a classical way by smoothing out the initial data and solving the inviscid system (
B0
). Afterwards, we should check that this procedure generates a family of approximated solutions which is bounded uniformly in the resolution space for a short uniform time. As to the strong convergence towards an exact solution to the initial value problem and the uniqueness follow in a straightforward way using similar arguments to
[
39
]
.

The second part of this paper seeks to address the same problem for the partial viscous system (
Bκ
) and especially we intend to investigate whether the results of Theorem
1.1
occur with uniform bounds for vanishing conductivity
\kappa.
As we shall see, this is a delicate task due to several technical points among which the compatibility assumption imposed for the density that could be scrambled by the diffusion effect. In addition, the approach developed to track the platitude degree is only conceived to work with the transport equations. Our main result related to this issue reads as follows and whose general statement can be found in Theorem
4.1
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(s,a)\in(0,1)\times[2,\infty)
. Consider a bounded domain
\Omega_{0}
of the plane whose boundary
\partial\Omega_{0}
is a Jordan curve of class
C^{1+s}
outside a closed countable set
\Sigma_{0}
. Let
\omega_{0}=1_{\Omega_{0}},\rho_{0}\in W^{2,a}\cap C^{2+s}
and we assume that
\nabla\rho_{0}
and
\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}
are identically zero on the singular set
\Sigma_{0}
. Then, there exists
T>0
independent of
\kappa\in[0,1]
for which the system (
Bκ
) admits a unique local solution
(\omega,\rho)
such that

\omega,\rho,\nabla\rho\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T],L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}),

with uniform bounds with respect to
\kappa\in[0,1].

Moreover, the velocity field
v
satisfies the estimate

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]\atop\kappa\in[0,1]}\frac{\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{h}^{c})}}{-\log h}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]),

where
\Sigma_{t}\triangleq\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0})
and
\Psi
denotes the viscous flow associated to
v
.
In addition, the boundary of
\Psi(t,\Omega_{0})

is locally in
C^{1+s^{\prime}}
outside the set
\Sigma_{t}
, for all
s^{\prime}<s
.

Before going over the main ideas of the proof, we shall make some comments.

Remark 1.1
.

(i)

Compared to the inviscid case discussed in Theorem
\ref{THEO:1:soft},
here we need to reinforce the regularity on the density and to strengthen the compatibility condition in order to balance some undesirable viscosity effects that we cannot overlook.

(ii)

Contrary to the inviscid case, where no assumptions are assumed for the singular set
\Sigma_{0}
, here we require that this set is composed with a countable family of points. This restriction can be significantly relaxed and replaced by the integrability assumption (
4.9
) which allows to cover more pathological singular sets.

(iii)

In the preceding theorem, we strengthen the regularity and the compatibility condition seen in Theorem
1.1
by assuming that
\rho_{0}\in C^{2+s}
and
\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\equiv\nabla\rho_{0}\equiv 0
on
\Sigma_{0}.
These constraints are needed to ensure the platitude assumption (
4.10
) required in
Proposition
\ref{Proposition visous}.
The degeneracy of the second derivative is one of the side effects brought by the dissipation which tends to diffuse the platitude information far away from the singular set. As to the regularity
\rho_{0}\in C^{2+s}

1
1

1

In fact, we need only to assume the
C^{2+s}
regularity on a small neighborhood of the singular set as it is more clear in the statement of Theorem
4.1
.
,
we believe that it is quite possible to keep the final results of Theorem
1.2
with the less restrictive assumption (
4.10
). It appears that with this latter condition we can derive all the a priori estimates, but in the existence part we need to reinforce the regularity assumption to generate a suitable approximation of the initial data that preserves the platitude condition (
4.10
). For more clarifications, we refer to Paragraph
\ref{sec:xx}.

(iv)

To complete our study, we shall prove the inviscid limit of the system (
Bκ
) towards (
B0
) when the diffusivity parameter goes to zero. In addition, we establish the rate of convergence of the velocities and the densities in Lebesgue spaces, as well as the convergence of the corresponding flows and the boundary parametrization of the patches outside the singular set.

Let us now outline the main ideas of the proof and sketch the major difficulties encountered in particular at the a priori estimates step.

\x⃝

L^{p}
-estimates for
\omega,\nabla\rho
.
This will handled in the proof of Proposition
4.1
through a suitable ansatz and appropriate estimates. The main issue concerns the compatibility condition; that is
\nabla\rho_{0}
and
\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}
vanish at the singular set
\Sigma_{0},
which is not easy to maintain during the motion due to the diffusion effects of the heat equation. When
\kappa=0,
we have already seen that the transport structure is helpful and one can track the platitude degree. In the current context, we should find another alternative to compile the advection and diffusion mechanisms which are in competition. This is considered as the main technical novelty of the paper where we shall use different tricks to deal with uniform estimates in transport-diffusion equation with singular potential. As we shall see, the key ingredients are a suitable decomposition for the quantity
\varrho\triangleq|\nabla\rho|^{2}
combined with an optimization arguments supplemented with De Giorgi-Nash estimates type. Let us briefly discuss the main lines to establish the results of Proposition
4.1
. First, by virtue of the PDE inequality (
4.17
) we infer

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\varrho(t,x)\leqslant 2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\varrho(t,x),

where the norm
\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}
is defined in (
2.2
) and the function
\ln^{+}
is stated in (
2.3
). Next we make the splitting

\varrho\triangleq(\bar{\varphi}+\psi)\eta,

where
\eta
is a positive function satisfying the PDE’s inequality

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+\big(v-2\kappa\nabla\log(\bar{\varphi}+\psi)\big)\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\eta\leqslant 0,&\\
\eta|_{t=0}=1,\end{array}\right.

the function
\bar{\varphi}
satisfies the transport equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)\bar{\varphi}(t,x)=2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\bar{\varphi}(t,x),&\\
\bar{\varphi}|_{t=0}=|\nabla\rho_{0}|^{2}\end{array}\right.

and
\psi
obeys the following transport-diffusion equation with a singular logarithmic potential, and subject to a vanishing initial data and a small forcing term

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\psi(t,x)=2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\psi(t,x)+\kappa\Delta\bar{\varphi},&\\
\psi|_{t=0}=0.\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

Applying the maximum principle we obtain that
\|\eta(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1
. The estimate of
\bar{\varphi}
can be handled as in the inviscid case and one may track the platitude degree. Actually, according to (
4.41
) one gets for an implicit short time
T>0

\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad\|\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}+\|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant C_{0}.

However, the estimate of
\psi
turns out to be more intricate due to the presence of the logarithmic potential. To counterbalance its short-range effect we use the smoothing effect of the heat equation together with the smallness of the source term
\kappa\Delta\bar{\varphi}
. Notice that the arguments vary depending on whether we are controlling
L^{p}
with
p
finite or infinite. In the first case, we implement an energy method based on a suitable integral decomposition related to the potential effect where we distinguish two regimes
d(x,\Sigma_{t})\leqslant\varepsilon
and
d(x,\Sigma_{t})\geqslant\varepsilon
. Roughly speaking, in the first region we use a smoothing effect leading to a control of type
\varepsilon^{\sigma}\kappa^{-1}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{p}}^{p},
for some
\sigma>0
related to the structure of the singular set
\Sigma_{0}
. Nevertheless, in the second region, we simply get a control of type
\ln(1/\varepsilon)\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}
. Then an optimization argument on
\varepsilon
leads to the desired estimate. The final estimates do not enable to conclude about the end limit case
p=\infty
due to the fast growth in
p
. To deal with this critical case we proceed with a second splitting of
\psi
into three parts reflecting different regimes, see (
4.51
). For the worst regime described by
\psi_{2}
and connected to the strong effect of the logarithmic potential, we use De Giorgi-Nash estimates stated in Lemma
A.4
.

Before achieving this discussion on the a priori estimates, let us point out the following observation related to the point raised before in Remark
1.1
-(iii). We do not claim any sort of optimality of the additional constraints on the regularity and the compatibility condition for the density as stated in Theorem
1.2
. We expect the result to hold with only the compatibility assumption of
Theorem
1.1
,
and we do believe that more refined analysis based on the maximal smoothing effects could be implemented. One of the main difficulties that we have encountered is connected to the
L^{p}
-estimates of
\psi
. The method that we have performed in the proof of Proposition
4.1
requires some extra-regularity on
\bar{\varphi}
acting as a source term in (
1.1
). As a matter of fact, according to the energy estimates, the control of
\nabla\bar{\varphi}
in
L^{p}
, for all
p\in[2,\infty]
seems to be mandatory to get the suitable estimates.

\x⃝
Co-normal regularity and loss estimates.
This part is technical and more involved than the inviscid case due to some strong viscosity effects in measuring the tangential regularity of the vorticity and the density. It will be performed following the strategy developed in
[
41
]
that we shall overview and isolate the main difficulties. First, the tangential regularity is associated with a suitable transport vector-field denoted here for the sake of simplicity by
X_{t}
; In principle, we should introduce a cut-off with a parameter
h>0
far away the singular set forcing to work with parametrized vector-fields. Thus from (
4.72
) we get

\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)\partial_{X_{t}}\omega=\partial_{X_{t}}\partial_{1}\rho.

Even though the velocity is not expected to be Lipschitz everywhere, the fact that the vector fields
X_{t}
are supported far away the singular set at a distance
h
with some degeneracy behavior, one can get a regularity persistence according to Proposition
2.4
. Then
to control
\partial_{X}\omega
in the negative Hölder space
C^{s-1}
, with
s\in(0,1)
, it is enough to get an estimate of
\|\partial_{X}\rho(t)\|_{C^{s}}.
This latter quantity introduces several technical issues. First, under Proposition
4.1
we can control at most
\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}
and there is no hope to improve it in general due to the lack of regularity of the velocity. The alternative for estimating
\|\partial_{X}\rho(t)\|_{C^{s}}
is to write down the dynamics governing the tangential regularity of the density stated in (
4.77
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\partial_{X_{t}}\rho=-\kappa[\Delta,\partial_{X_{t}}]\rho&\\
\partial_{X_{t}}\rho_{|_{t=0}}=\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}.\end{array}\right.

(1.2)

At this stage, the delicate point is to estimate the commutator which induces a sensitive loss of regularity and the main scope is to explore whether the heat equation can remedy to this defect through its maximal smoothing effect. For this aim, we proceed with a suitable decomposition of the commutator using para-differential calculus and perform two sorts of maximal smoothing effects: the first one is inherited from the transport-diffusion part in (
1.2
) and the second one stems from the density equation. We point out that the smoothing effects are established only with Besov spaces constructed over Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}
with
p<\infty,
see Proposition
2.5
. Notice that there is an apparent frequency logarithmic loss of regularity connected to the low regularity of the velocity. This has a direct impact on the regularity persistence as stated in Proposition
4.2
. Actually, we are only able to propagate estimates with some time loss and this explains in part the loss of regularity observed for the boundary in Theorem
1.2
. Another remark to state concerns the endpoint case
p=\infty
which seems to be completely out of reach and it is left open. To cover this case, we could be tempted to use the Lagrangian coordinates method developed successfully in
[
40
]
, but unfortunately, it seems to work only when the velocity is Lipschitz, which is not the case here.

✦
Outline of the paper
. In Section
2
, we shall introduce a package of various notations freely used throughout this work. Afterward, we state some results on Littlewood-Paley theory, singular vortex patches, function spaces related to the co-normal regularity of the vorticity, logarithmic estimate and we end with a discussion on the maximal smoothing effects for transport-diffusion equations governed by a
\log
-Lipschitz velocity. In Section
3
, we study the inviscid case governed by (
B0
) where we prove a general version of Theorem
1.1
, see Theorem
3.1
. The proof will be performed into several steps, from the
L^{p}
a priori estimates for the vorticity and the gradient of the density, see Proposition
3.1
, to the propagation of anisotropic regularity. Section
4
deals with the viscous system (
Bκ
) and aims at proving the general statement of Theorem
1.2
, described through Theorem
4.1
. In section
5
, we study the inviscid limit of (
Bκ
) towards (
B0
) when the diffusivity parameter goes to zero. In the final Section
A
, we collect some technical results used along the paper.

2.
Techniques related to the vortex patch problem

The main concern of this introductory section is to collect some concepts and discuss useful tools required in studying the vortex patch problem. We distinguish two important parts. The first one covers some results on the functional material centered around some aspects of the para-differential calculus, classical Besov spaces, and anisotropic Besov spaces. The second one focuses on the regularity persistence and the maximal smoothing effects for transport-diffusion equations when the drift is given by a velocity field that scales slightly below the Lipschitz class. This is typically the case for vortex patches whose singular points are of corners type.

2.1.
Notation

For the convenience of the reader, we begin with introducing the main notation and conventions that will be used frequently in the sequel of this paper.

•

We shall use the notation
A\lesssim B
to denote
A\leqslant CB
, for some constant
C>0
that is independent of the main quantities of the problem, namely, which is independent of
A
and
B
. This constant is also allowed to differ from line to another.

•

We use
C_{0}
(resp.
C_{0,T}
) to denote a constant depending on the initial data of the problem (resp. and on
T
). This constant may vary from line to line.

•

The notation
f\triangleq g
means that
f
equals to
g
by definition.

•

The commutator between two operators
A
and
B
is defined by

[A,B]\triangleq AB-BA.

•

For any non–empty subset
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the characteristic function of
F
, denoted by
{\bf 1}_{F}
, is defined by

{\bf 1}_{F}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\textrm{if $x\in F$,}\\
0&\textrm{if $x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus F$.}\end{array}\right.

•

For any
h\geqslant 0
and for any non–empty subset
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we denote

F_{h}\triangleq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\;d(x,F)\leqslant h\right\},\quad F_{h}^{c}\triangleq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\;d(x,F)>h\right\},

(2.1)

with
d(x,F)
is the distance of
x
to
F
given by

d(x,F)=\inf_{y\in F}d(x,y).

We remark that this function is
1
-Lipschitz, which implies that its derivative in the distribution sense is bounded, with

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla d(\cdot,F)|\leqslant 1.

•

For any non-empty subset
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and for any vector-field
v:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
we define

\|v(t)\|_{L(F)}\triangleq\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}\frac{\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(F_{h}^{c})}}{-\log h}.

(2.2)

•

We define the function
\ln^{+}:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow[1,\infty)
through

\ln^{+}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\ln(t),\quad\hbox{if}\quad 0<t\leqslant e^{-1}\\
1,\quad\hbox{if}\quad t\geqslant e^{-1}.\end{array}\right.

(2.3)

•

For any non–empty set
\Sigma_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define the functions
\chi
and
\varphi_{0}
by

\chi(\tau)\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau,\quad\hbox{if}\quad\tau\leqslant e^{-1}\\
e^{-1},\quad\hbox{if}\quad\tau\geqslant e^{-1}\end{array}\right.\quad\hbox{and}\quad\varphi_{0}(x)\triangleq\chi\big(d(x,\Sigma_{0})\big),\;\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(2.4)

2.2.
Functional toolbox

In this section, we intend to gather some useful tools and state classical results related to frequency localization and functional spaces. Some aspects of para-differential calculus will be explored at the end.

Consider a radial function decreasing function
\chi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a reference cut-off function, monotonically decaying along rays and so that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\chi\equiv 1&\textrm{if $|\xi|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}$,}\\
0\leqslant\chi\leqslant 1&\textrm{if $\frac{1}{2}\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant 1$,}\\
\chi\equiv 0&\textrm{if $|\xi|\geqslant 1.$}\end{array}\right.

Define
\varphi(\xi)\triangleq\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{2})-\chi(\xi),
then we have
\varphi\geqslant 0,\,\textnormal{supp}\varphi\subset\mathcal{C}\triangleq\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\frac{1}{2}\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant 1\}
, and

\forall\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\chi(\xi)+\sum_{q\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi(2^{-q}\xi)=1.

The Littlewood-Paley operators are defined as follows: for every
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\Delta_{-1}v\triangleq\chi(\sqrt{-\Delta})v,\quad\forall\,q\in\mathbb{N},\quad\Delta_{q}v\triangleq\varphi(2^{-q}\sqrt{-\Delta})v\quad\hbox{and}\quad S_{q}v\triangleq\sum_{j\leqslant q-1}\Delta_{j}v.

The first useful application is the decomposition

v=\sum_{q\geq-1}\Delta_{q}v,\quad\text{in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Moreover, the operators
\Delta_{q}
and
S_{q}
enjoy the following properties, valid for all
u,v
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(i)

|p-q|\geq 2\Longrightarrow\Delta_{p}\Delta_{q}v\equiv 0
,

(ii)

|p-q|\geq 4\Longrightarrow\Delta_{q}(S_{p-1}u\Delta_{p}v)\equiv 0
,

(iii)

\Delta_{q},S_{q}:L^{m}\longrightarrow L^{m}
uniformly with respect to
q\geqslant-1
and
m\in[1,\infty]
.

Next, we recall the well-known Bernstein inequalities, see for instance
[
4
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for
1\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant\infty
, for every function
v
and every
q,k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\|\partial^{\alpha}S_{q}v\|_{L^{b}}\leqslant C^{k}2^{q\big(k+2\big(\frac{1}{a}-\frac{1}{b}\big)\big)}\|S_{q}v\|_{L^{a}},\\

C^{-k}2^{qk}\|\Delta_{q}v\|_{L^{a}}\leqslant\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\|\partial^{\alpha}\Delta_{q}v\|_{L^{a}}\leqslant C^{k}2^{qk}\|\Delta_{q}v\|_{L^{a}}.

Besov spaces are defined below.

Definition 2.1
.

For
(s,p,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,\infty]^{2}
. The non-homogeneous Besov space
B_{p,r}^{s}
is the set of all the tempered distributions
v\in\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}
such that

\displaystyle\|v\|_{{B}_{p,r}^{s}}\triangleq\|2^{qs}\|\Delta_{q}v\|_{L^{p}}\|_{\ell^{r}(q\geqslant-1)}<\infty.

Remark 2.1
.

In the case
p=q=\infty,\;k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
the Besov space
B_{\infty,\infty}^{k+\alpha}
coincides with the Hölder space
C^{k+\alpha}
. More precisely, we have

\|v\|_{{B}_{\infty,\infty}^{k+\alpha}}\lesssim\|v\|_{C^{k+\alpha}}\triangleq\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sup_{\underset{|\alpha|=k}{x\neq y}}\tfrac{|\partial^{\alpha}v(x)-\partial^{\alpha}v(y)|}{|x-y|^{s}}\lesssim\|v\|_{{B}_{\infty,\infty}^{k+\alpha}}.

(2.5)

To simplify the notations in some estimates, we will also use the notation

\|v\|_{{s}}\triangleq\|v\|_{C^{s}},\quad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

Let us now recall the definition of the Log-Lipschitz space.

Definition 2.2
.

We denote by
{LL}
the space of log-Lipschitz functions, that is the set of functions
v:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying

\|v\|_{LL}\triangleq\underset{0<|x-y|<1}{\sup}\frac{|v(x)-v(y)|}{|x-y|\log\frac{e}{|x-y|}}<\infty.

The following classical estimate is very useful. It is a simple consequence of the embedding
B_{\infty,\infty}^{1}\subset LL
combined with Bernstein inequality and the Biot–Savart law

v(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{(x-y)^{\perp}}{|x-y|^{2}}\omega(y)dy,x^{\perp}=(-x_{2},x_{1}).

(2.6)

Lemma 2.2
.

[
4
, Chapter 7]

Let
v
be a vector field given by (
2.6
). Then, for any
a\in[1,\infty)
, we have

\|v\|_{LL}\leqslant C\|\omega\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}},

with
C
depending only on
a
.

The characterization below of the space
LL
is useful.

Proposition 2.1
.

[
4
, Proposition 2.111]
For any function
v\in LL
, we have

\displaystyle\|v\|_{LL}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{q\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\|\nabla S_{q}v\|_{L^{\infty}}}{2+q}\lesssim\|v\|_{LL}

and for all
q\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{q}v\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|v\|_{LL}(2+q)2^{-q}.

The well-known
Bony’s
decomposition
[
4
]
enables us to split formally the product of two tempered distributions
u
and
v
into three pieces. In
what follows, we shall adopt the following definition

Definition 2.3
.

For a given
u,v\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
we have

uv=T_{u}v+T_{v}u+R(u,v),

with

T_{u}v=\sum_{q}S_{q-1}u\Delta_{q}v,\quad R(u,v)=\sum_{q}\Delta_{q}u\widetilde{\Delta}_{q}v\quad\hbox{and}\quad\widetilde{\Delta}_{q}=\Delta_{q-1}+\Delta_{q}+\Delta_{q+1}.

The following lemma provides some useful product laws in Besov spaces.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(s,s_{1},s_{2},p)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0)\times(0,\infty)\times[1,\infty].
Let
T
and
R
be the operators given by Definition
2.3
. Then, the following estimates hold.

\|T_{u}v\|_{B^{s_{1}+s}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|u\|_{B^{s_{1}}_{p,\infty}}\|v\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}}.\vskip 5.69054pt

\|T_{u}v\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}.\vskip 5.69054pt

\|R(u,v)\|_{B^{s_{2}}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{B^{s_{2}}_{p,\infty}}.

The proof of Lemma
2.3
is elementary, see for instance
[
4
, Chapter 2]
. As a corollary, one obtains the following useful estimate.

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
(s,p)\in(0,1)\times[1,\infty]
and
X
be a vector field belonging to
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
as well as its divergence. Let
f
be a Lipschitz scalar function. Then, for
j\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\|(\partial_{x_{j}}X)\cdot\nabla f\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,\infty}}\leqslant C\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}}\big(\|\textnormal{div}X\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}+\|X\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\big).

The proof of Corollary
2.1
is based on Lemma
2.3
. One may see also
[
39
]
for a detailed proof in the case
p=\infty
, thereby, the general case
p\in[1,\infty]
can be treated similarly.

We conclude this section by recalling two lemmas that will be helpful in the proof of Theorem
1.2
. For more details on the proof of the first one, we refer to
[
41
, Lemma 5]
. Meanwhile, the second one is based on
[
41
, Lemma 7]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(s,p)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[1,\infty]
and
K
be a compact in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
d\geqslant 1
. There exists a constant
C_{K}
such that, for all tempered distribution
v
in
C^{s}
, supported in
K
, we have

\|v\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\leqslant C_{K}\|v\|_{s}.

Lemma 2.5
.

There exists a partition
(\mathscr{O}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for
d\geqslant 1
, and a sequence of positive smooth functions
(\psi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}
satisfying

(i)

\textnormal{supp}\;\psi_{n}\subset\mathscr{O}_{n},
with
|\mathscr{O}_{n}|\lesssim 1
,

(ii)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}\psi_{n}(x)=1
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(iii)

there exists
N_{d}>0
such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\textnormal{card}\Big\{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}:x\in\mathscr{O}_{n}\Big\}\leqslant N_{d}
,

(iv)

for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, we have that

\|\nabla^{\alpha}\psi_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C_{\alpha,p},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}.

(2.7)

For the convenience of the reader, we present below a short self-contained proof of Lemma
2.5
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we only outline the idea of the proof in the two dimensional case
d=2.
Let us introduce the following notations, for
r>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
, we denote

\widetilde{B}_{r}\triangleq B(0,r),\quad\widetilde{B}_{r,n}\triangleq\widetilde{B}_{r}+x_{n}.

Above, the sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N^{\star}}}
is such that
\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}\{x_{n}\}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}.
Moreover, we have

•

The collection
(\widetilde{B}_{2,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is a recovery of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying

|\widetilde{B}_{2,n}|=|\widetilde{B}_{2}|=4\pi.

•

There exists
N>0
such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have that
\textnormal{card}\big\{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}:x\in\widetilde{B}_{2,n}\big\}\leqslant N.

Now, consider a smooth function
\varphi
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with values in
[0,1]
such that

\varphi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\text{if }x\in\widetilde{B}_{1},\\
0,&\text{if }x\notin\widetilde{B}_{2}.\end{array}\right.

Thereby, let us define the sequence of functions
\varphi_{n}
as

\varphi_{n}(x)\triangleq\varphi(x-x_{n}).

Remark that, by construction,
\varphi_{n}
takes its values in
[0,1]
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and moreover we have that

\textnormal{supp}\,\varphi_{n}\subset\widetilde{B}_{2,n},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}

and

\|\nabla^{\alpha}\varphi_{n}\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim C_{\alpha,p},\quad\forall(p,\alpha,n)\in[1,\infty]\times\mathbb{N}^{2}\times\mathbb{N}^{\star}.

Thereafter, we define the function
\phi
as

\phi(x)\triangleq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}\varphi_{n}(x).

Observe that
\phi
is well-defined and smooth since the above sum is in fact a finite sum containing at most
N
terms. Furthermore, we have, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

1\leqslant\phi(x)\leqslant N.

Now, we define the partition of unity
\psi_{n}
as

\psi_{n}\triangleq\frac{\varphi_{n}}{\phi}.

Hence, it is easy to check that
\psi_{n}
is smooth for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and satisfies all the required claims of Lemma
2.5
.
∎

2.3.
Vortex patch formalism

The main goal in this subsection is twofold. First, we collect some valuable results on the set dynamics inclusion for a flow associated with a log-Lipschitz velocity. Second, we recall some definitions related to the anisotropic Besov and Hölder spaces frequently used to track the co-normal regularity of the patch. We end this short discussion with a logarithmic estimate which plays a crucial role, in particular in the vortex patch formalism. More details can be found in
[
17
,
39
,
41
]
.

The flow associated to a vector field
v
is defined by the mapping
\Psi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
which satisfies the following integral equation

\forall\,t\geqslant 0,\,\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\Psi(t,x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}v\big(\tau,\Psi(\tau,x)\big)d\tau.

First, we embark with the following result which describes the dynamics of a given set through the flow associated with a
LL
vector field. The complete proof can be found in
[
17
]
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
A_{0}
be a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2},v
be a vector field belonging to
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};{LL})
and
\Psi
its flow. Then, by setting
A(t)\triangleq\Psi(t,A_{0})
we get in view of notation (
2.1
)

\displaystyle\Psi\big(t,(A_{0})_{h}^{c}\big)\subset\big(A(t)\big)^{c}_{\delta_{t}(h)},\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\delta_{t}(h)\triangleq h^{\exp\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}.

(2.8)

For all
0\leqslant\tau\leqslant t
,

\Psi\big(\tau,\Psi^{-1}\big(t,(A_{t})_{h}^{c})\big)\subset\big(A(\tau)\big)^{c}_{\delta_{\tau,t}(h)},\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\delta_{\tau,t}(h)\triangleq h^{\exp\int_{\tau}^{t}\|v(\tau^{\prime})\|_{LL}d\tau^{\prime}}.

As a consequence, we state the following elementary lemma which will be of a constant use in different situations that will be developed in this paper.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\Psi
denote the flow associated with a log-Lipschitz vector field
v\in L^{1}([0,T];LL)
, for some
T>0
. Let
\Sigma_{0}
be a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and denote

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}\triangleq\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0}).

(2.9)

Then for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there holds

\ln^{+}\left(d(x,\Sigma_{t})\right)\leqslant e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}\ln^{+}\left[d(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0})\right].

Proof.

From Lemma
2.6
we find for
h\leqslant e^{-1}

d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)\geqslant h\Longrightarrow d\big(x,\Sigma_{t}\big)\geqslant h^{\exp\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}.

Then using the decreasing property of
t\mapsto\ln^{+}t,
combined with

\ln^{+}(t^{m})\leqslant m\ln^{+}(t),\quad\forall t>0,\quad\forall m\geqslant 1,\vskip 2.84526pt

\ln^{+}\left(\min\{t,e^{-1}\}\right)=\ln^{+}(t),\quad\forall t>0,

and the particular choice
h=\min\{d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big),e^{-1}\}
we obtain the desired inequality. This achieves the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now, let us setup the main tools required in the study the vortex patch problems. For more details about this subject, we refer to
[
17
]
and the references therein.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(s,p)\in(0,1)\times[1,\infty]
, then for any closed set
\Sigma
of the plane and any family of vector fields
\mathcal{X}=(X_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
belonging to
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
as well as their divergence, we define the quantities

I(\Sigma,X)\triangleq\inf_{x\notin\Sigma}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|X_{\lambda}(x)|,

\widetilde{\|}X_{\lambda}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\triangleq\|X_{\lambda}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}+\|\textnormal{div}X_{\lambda}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}

and

N_{s,p}(\Sigma,X)\triangleq\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\frac{\widetilde{\|}X_{\lambda}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}}{I(\Sigma,X)}.

For each element
X_{\lambda}
of the previous family we define its action on bounded real-valued functions
u
in the weak sense as

\partial_{X_{\lambda}}u\triangleq\textnormal{div}(u\,X_{\lambda})-u\,\textnormal{div}X_{\lambda}.

Furthermore, for all
k\in\mathbb{N},
we set

\|u\|^{s+k,p}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{X}}\triangleq N_{s,p}(\Sigma,\mathcal{X})\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant k}\|\partial^{\alpha}u\|_{L^{\infty}}+\displaystyle{\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}}\,\frac{\|\partial_{X_{\lambda}}u\|_{B^{s+k-1}_{p,\infty}}}{I(\Sigma,\mathcal{X})}.

(2.10)

In the particular case
p=\infty
, we simplify the notation by removing the exponent
p
from the definitions. That is for instance, we use the notation

\|u\|^{s+k}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{X}}\triangleq\|u\|^{s+k,\infty}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{X}}.

(2.11)

The Besov space
B_{\Sigma,\mathcal{X}}^{s+k,p}
(respectively,
C_{\Sigma,\mathcal{X}}^{s+k}
) is then defined as the set of functions
u
in
W^{k,\infty}
such that

\|u\|^{s+k,p}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{X}}
(respectively,
\|u\|^{s+k}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{X}}
) is finite.

Let us now introduce a more accurate definition of the admissible vector fields that we will use in the sequel.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
be a triplet of non-negative real numbers. We consider a family
\mathcal{X}=(X_{\lambda,h})_{(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}]}
of vector fields belonging to
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
as well as their divergences, with
s\in(0,1)
and we denote by
\mathcal{X}_{h}=(X_{\lambda,h})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. The family
\mathcal{X}
will be said
\Sigma-
admissible of order
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
if and only if the following properties are satisfied:

\forall(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}],\ \textnormal{supp}X_{\lambda,h}\subset\Sigma_{h^{\alpha}}^{c},

\inf_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{-\gamma}I(\Sigma_{h},\mathcal{X}_{h})>0

and

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}N_{s,p}(\Sigma_{h},\mathcal{X}_{h})<\infty.

Some important remarks are in order.

Remark 2.2
.

Notice that in the pioneering work
[
17
]
, Chemin sets up the above formalism only in the Hölder case
p=\infty
which fits well with the regularity persistence for the transported singular vortex patch associated to Euler equations. However, in the viscous case
[
41
]
additional technical issues emerge forcing to consider a family of vector fields
(X_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
belonging to Besov classes
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
, with
p<\infty
. The main difficulty behind this restriction stems from the maximal smoothing effects in the density equation advected by a velocity field which scales below the Lipschitz class, see also
Remark
\ref{remark:p<infini}.

Remark 2.3
.

Concretely, the family of vector fields
\mathcal{X}
that we shall work with vanishes near the singular set and therefore we should assume that
\gamma,\beta>0
. Albeit the parameter
\alpha>1
.

The next result deals with a logarithmic estimate established in
[
17
]
which is the main key in the study of the generalized vortex patches. More precisely, we have the following estimate.

Theorem 2.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
C
such that for any
a\in[1,\infty)
and
s\in(0,1)
the following holds.
Let
\Sigma
be a closed set of the plane and
X
be a family of vector fields. Consider a function
\omega\in C^{s}_{\Sigma,X}\cap L^{a}
and let
v
be the divergence-free vector field with vorticity
\omega
, then we have

\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma^{c})}\leqslant Ca\|\omega\|_{L^{a}}+\frac{C}{s}\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}\log\Big(e+\frac{\|\omega\|^{s}_{\Sigma,X}}{\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}}\Big).

Above, the norm
\|\cdot\|^{s}_{\Sigma,X}
is given by (
2.11
).

Now we discuss some important results for solutions to transport equations.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\Sigma_{0}
be a closed set in the plane,
(p,h)\in[1,\infty]\times(0,e^{-1}]
and
v
be a smooth divergence-free vector field with vorticity
\omega\triangleq\nabla\times v
. Then, using the notations (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) the following assertions hold.
Let
f
be a solution of the transport equation on
[0,T]

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla f=g,&\\
f_{|t=0}=f_{0},\end{array}\right.

(T)

where, we assume that
\textnormal{supp}\,f_{0}\subset(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
. Let
t\in[0,T]\mapsto s_{t}\in(-1,1)
be a decreasing function,
\delta_{t}(h)
be defined as in (
2.8
) and let
g=g_{1}+g_{2}
be given such that
\textnormal{supp}\,g(t)\subset(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}(h)}^{c}
for any
t\in[0,T]
and we suppose that
g_{2}
satisfies

\|g_{2}(t)\|_{B^{s_{t}}_{p,\infty}}\leqslant-C\log h\;W(t)\|f(t)\|_{B^{s_{t}}_{p,\infty}},\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

for some universal constant
C>0
, where
W(t)
is given by

W(t)\triangleq\big(\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}+\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}}\big)\exp\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\bigg).

(2.12)

Then, we have

\|f(t)\|_{B^{s_{t}}_{p,\infty}}\leqslant\|f_{0}\|_{B^{s_{0}}_{p,\infty}}h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}+\int_{0}^{t}h^{-C\int_{\tau}^{t}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}\|g_{1}(\tau)\|_{B^{s_{\tau}}_{p,\infty}}d\tau,\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

The proof of Proposition
2.2
is straightforward, we refer to
[
17
,
40
]
for more details. As a consequence, we get the following corollary that will useful in this paper.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
(s,h,a,p)\in(0,1)\times(0,e^{-1}]\times(1,\infty)\times[1,\infty]
. Consider a non-increasing function
t\in[0,T]\mapsto\sigma_{t}\in(0,s]
. Let
\Sigma_{0}
be a closed set of the plane and
X_{0}
be a vector field of class
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
as well as its divergence and whose support is embedded in
(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
. Let
v
be a divergence-free vector field with vorticity
\omega
belonging to
L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}.
If
X_{t}
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)X_{t}=\partial_{X_{t}}v,&\\
X_{|t=0}=X_{0},\end{array}\right.

(2.13)

then, the following assertions hold

\textnormal{supp}X_{t}\subset\big(\Sigma_{t}\big)_{\delta_{t}(h)}^{c},

\|\textnormal{div }X_{t}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\leqslant\|\textnormal{div }X_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\|}X_{t}\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}\Big(\widetilde{\|}X_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}+C\int_{0}^{t}h^{C\int_{0}^{\tau}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\omega(\tau)\|_{B^{\sigma_{\tau}-1}_{p,\infty}}d\tau\Big),

(2.15)

where
W(t)
and
\delta_{t}(h)
are, respectively, defined by (
2.12
) and (
2.8
).

Proof.

The result of the support of
X_{t}
can be deduced from Lemma
2.6
and the complete proof can be found in
[
17
]
. The estimate (
2.14
) follows by applying Proposition
2.2
to the equation

\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)\textnormal{div }X_{t}=0.

For the last estimate (
2.15
), we shall admit the following assertion and we refer to
[
17
, Chapter 9]
and
[
41
, Lemma 12]
for more details. For all
t\in[0,T]
, we have that

\partial_{X_{t}}v(t)=g_{1}(t)+g_{2}(t),

(2.16)

with

\displaystyle\|g_{1}(t)\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\|\partial_{X_{t}}\omega(t)\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}-1}_{p,\infty}}+C\|\textnormal{div}X_{t}\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}}\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}

(2.17)

and

\displaystyle\|g_{2}(t)\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle-C\log h\;W(t)\|X_{t}\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}}.

Then, applying once again Proposition
2.2
to the equation (
2.13
), implies

\displaystyle\|X_{t}\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|X_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\textnormal{div }X_{\tau}\|_{B^{\sigma_{\tau}}_{p,\infty}}\|\omega(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}h^{-C\int_{\tau}^{t}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}d\tau

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\omega(\tau)\|_{B^{\sigma_{t}-1}_{p,\infty}}h^{-C\int_{\tau}^{t}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}d\tau.

According to the definition of
W(t)
, we can write

\int_{0}^{t}\|\omega(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}d\tau\leqslant h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau},

together with (
2.14
) and the embedding
B^{s}_{p,\infty}\hookrightarrow B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}
for all
t\in[0,T]
,
yield to (
2.15
). Therefore, Corollary
2.2
is proved.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

A localized version of (
2.15
) is given by

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}X_{t}\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}X_{0}\|_{L^{p}}+C\int_{0}^{t}2^{-j\sigma_{\tau}}\|\textnormal{div}X_{\tau}\|_{B^{\sigma_{\tau}}_{p,\infty}}W(\tau)d\tau+C\int_{0}^{t}2^{-j\sigma_{\tau}}\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\omega\|_{B^{\sigma_{\tau}-1}_{p,\infty}}d\tau

(2.18)

\displaystyle+C(-\log h)\int_{0}^{t}2^{-j\sigma_{\tau}}\|X_{\tau}\|_{B^{\sigma_{\tau}}_{p,\infty}}W(\tau)d\tau

which comes from the decomposition (
2.16
) and the estimate (
2.17
). Estimate (
2.18
) will be used later in the proof of Proposition
4.2
related to the viscous case.

We conclude this part by the following proposition which aims to study the dynamic of an admissible family of vector fields away from the singular set. For the convenience of the reader, we also recall the details of the proof from
[
17
,
39
]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
v
be a smooth divergence-free vector field and let
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\big(X_{0,\lambda,h}\big)_{\lambda\in\Lambda,h\in(0,e^{-1}]}
be a
\Sigma_{0}-
admissible family of vector fields. We define the time dependent vector fields
\big(X_{t,\lambda,h}\big)_{\lambda\in\Lambda,h\in(0,e^{-1}]}
by

X_{t,\lambda,h}(x)\triangleq\big(\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi\big)\big(t,\Psi^{-1}(t,x)\big),\quad\forall\,t\geqslant 0,\quad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

where
\Psi
is the flow associated with the vector field
v
. Then we have

I\big((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t})_{h}\big)\geqslant I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}\big)h^{\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau},

(2.19)

where

\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)\triangleq h^{\exp(-\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau)}

(2.20)

and
W(t)
is given by (
2.12
) and
I
is introduced in Definition
2.4
.

Proof.

From the definition of
X_{t,\lambda,h}
, one gets

Y_{t,\lambda,h}(x)\triangleq X_{t,\lambda,h}\big(\Psi(t,x)\big)=\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi(t,x),

and clearly we have

\partial_{t}Y_{t,\lambda,h}(x)=\{\nabla v(t,\Psi(t,x))\}\cdot Y_{t,\lambda,h}(x).

Fix
t>0
and set for
\tau\in[0,t]
,
Z(\tau,x)=Y_{t-\tau,\lambda,h}(x)
, then

\partial_{\tau}Z(\tau,x)=-\{\nabla v(t-\tau,\Psi(t-\tau,x))\}\cdot Z(\tau,x).

Gronwall lemma provides us

\displaystyle|Z(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle|Z(0,x)|\,e^{\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla v(t-\tau,\Psi(t-\tau,x))|d\tau}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle|Z(0,x)|\,e^{\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla v(\tau,\Psi(\tau,x))|d\tau},

which is equivalent to

|Y_{0,\lambda,h}(x)|\leqslant|Y_{t,\lambda,h}(x)|e^{\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla v(\tau,\Psi(\tau,x))|d\tau}.

This gives in turn,

|X_{0,\lambda,h}(\Psi^{-1}(t,x))|\leqslant|X_{t,\lambda,h}(x)|\,e^{\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla v(\tau,\Psi(\tau,\Psi^{-1}(t,x)))|d\tau}.

Hence, we infer that

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|X_{0,\lambda,h}(\Psi^{-1}(t,x))|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|X_{t,\lambda,h}(x)|

(2.21)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\big\|\nabla v\big(\tau,\Psi(\tau,\Psi^{-1}(t,\cdot))\big)\big\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c})}d\tau\Big),

where we recall that
\Sigma_{t}=\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0})
and
(\Sigma_{t})^{c}_{\eta}=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2};\,d\big(x,\Sigma_{t}\big)\geqslant\eta\big\}
. According to the second point in Lemma
2.6
one deduces that

\Psi^{-1}\big(t,\big(\Sigma_{t}\big)_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c}\big)\subset\big(\Sigma_{0}\big)_{\delta_{t}(\delta_{t}^{-1}(h))}^{c}=(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}.

Then, we immediately find in accordance with (
2.21
)

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|X_{0,\lambda,h}(\Psi^{-1}(t,x))|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in\Psi^{-1}(t,(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c})}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|X_{0,\lambda,h}(y)|

(2.22)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|X_{0,\lambda,h}(y)|

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}\big).

Moreover, in view of the same lemma we have

\Psi\Big(\tau,\Psi^{-1}\big(t,(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c}\big)\Big)\subset\big(\Sigma_{\tau}\big)_{\delta_{\tau,t}(\delta_{t}^{-1}(h))}^{c}=\big(\Sigma_{\tau}\big)_{\delta_{\tau}^{-1}(h)}^{c}\subset\big(\Sigma_{\tau}\big)_{h}^{c}.

Consequently, we may write

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla v\big(\tau,\Psi(\tau,\Psi^{-1}(t,\cdot))\big)\big\|_{L^{\infty}\big(({\Sigma_{t}})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c}\big)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\nabla v(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{\tau})_{h}^{c})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-(\log h)\|\nabla v(\tau)\|_{L(\Sigma_{\tau})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-(\log h)W(\tau).

Combining the last estimate with (
2.21
) and(
2.22
) yields

I\big((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t})_{h}\big)\geqslant I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}\big)h^{\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}.

This ends the proof of the estimate (
2.19
).
∎

2.4.
Maximal smoothing effects with rough drift

In this paragraph, we shall explore some a priori estimates for transport-diffusion equations governed by a
\log-
Lipschitz velocity. They are considered as one of the cornerstone tools in tracking the co-normal regularity of the vorticity and the density with respect to a judicious transported vector-fields, see in particular Proposition
3.2
and Proposition
4.2
. We first start with recalling in Proposition
2.4
below some important results related to the regularity persistence in the transport-diffusion equation as well as the maximal smoothing effects. Then, in Proposition
2.5
we shall provide specific smoothing effects with a source term, extending a previous result established in
[
41
]
. We make a slight adaptation of the same method using Lagrangian coordinates and para-differential calculus. Finally, in Corollary
2.3
we formulate our main result of this section related to the maximal smoothing effects with a frequency logarithmic loss. This apparent loss seems to be unavoidable due to the lack of regularity of the velocity field which scales slightly below the Lipschitz class. For a detailed proof, we refer to
[
17
,
41
]
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
(s,r,p,a)\in(-1,1)\times[1,\infty]\times(1,\infty)\times(1,\infty)
and
v
be a smooth divergence-free vector field. Then, with the notations (
2.8
), (
2.9
) and (
2.12
), the following assertions hold true.
Let
f\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T],B^{s}_{p,\infty}\big)
be a solution of the transport-diffusion model

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla f-\kappa\Delta f={\bf F}+\kappa{\bf G},&\\
f_{|t=0}=f_{0}.\end{array}\right.

(TD
κ
)

Assume that
\textnormal{supp}f(t)\subset(\Sigma_{t})^{c}_{\delta_{t}(h)}
with
h\in(0,e^{-1}]
. Then, for every
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim C_{p}h^{{-C_{s}}\int_{0}^{t}A(\tau)d\tau}\Big(\|f_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}+\big(\kappa^{-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}+t^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}\big)\|{\bf F}\|_{\widetilde{L}^{r}_{t}B^{s-\frac{2}{r^{\prime}}}_{p,\infty}}+\big(1+\kappa t\big)\|{{\bf G}}\|_{\widetilde{L}^{\infty}_{t}B^{s-2}_{p,\infty}}\Big),

where,
\delta_{t}(h)
is defined in (
2.8
) and

A(t)=\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau},

C_{p}=\frac{p^{2}}{p-1},\quad C_{s}=\frac{1}{1-s^{2}},\quad r^{\prime}=\frac{r}{r-1}.

More precisely, we have for all
q\geqslant-1

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{q}f(t)\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C_{p}\|\Delta_{q}f_{0}\|_{L^{p}}+C_{p}\big(\kappa^{-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}+t^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}\big)2^{-\frac{2q}{r^{\prime}}}\|{\Delta_{q}\bf F}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}+C_{p}\big(1+\kappa t\big)2^{-2q}\|{\Delta_{q}{\bf G}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{p}}

(2.23)

\displaystyle-C_{p}\log h\int_{0}^{t}C_{s}2^{-qs}\|f(\tau)\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}A(\tau)d\tau.

Remark 2.5
.

Proposition
2.4
requires
p<\infty
because of the constant
C_{p}
which is unbounded as
p
tends to infinity. This technical issue is relevant to the viscous case which forces us to study the vortex patch problem in Besov spaces with integrability index
p<\infty
contrary to the inviscid case given by Proposition
2.2
where we have no obstruction on the index
p
.

As already mentioned, we state the pivotal result of this part which focuses on another smoothing effect type for a transport-diffusion equation with a source term. In contrast with Proposition
2.4
, the proposition stated below can be extended to the borderline case
p=\infty
using the techniques of Lagrangian coordinates in the spirit of the work
[
41
]
developed for the 2d Navier-Stokes equations. More precisely, we will prove.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
p,m\in[1,\infty],r\in[m,\infty]
and
T>0
. Consider a divergence-free vector field
v
belonging to
L^{1}([0,T];LL),f_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\mathtt{F}\in L^{m}([0,T];L^{p})
. Let
f
be a solution to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla f-\kappa\Delta f=\mathtt{F}&\\
f_{|t=0}=f_{0}\end{array}\right.

(2.24)

that satisfies

\|f(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant G(t),\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(2.25)

for some increasing function
G:[0,T]\to[0,\infty)
.
Then, for any
t\in[0,T]
and for any
q\geqslant-1

(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|\Delta_{q}f\|_{L_{t}^{r}L^{p}}\lesssim G(t)\Big(1+\kappa t+(q+2)\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}}+(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{m}-1}\|{\Delta_{q}\mathtt{F}}\|_{L^{m}_{t}L^{p}}.

Proof.

We localize
f
by applying the operator
\Delta_{q}
to (
2.24
), then by setting
f_{q}=\Delta_{q}f
and
\mathtt{F}_{q}=\Delta_{q}\mathtt{F}
one gets

\partial_{t}f_{q}+S_{q-1}v\cdot\nabla f_{q}-\kappa\Delta f_{q}=S_{q-1}v\cdot\nabla f_{q}-\Delta_{q}(v\cdot\nabla f)+\mathtt{F}_{q}\triangleq{\bf R}_{q}+\mathtt{F}_{q}.

(2.26)

Let us denote by
\Psi_{q}(t)
the flow associated to the regularized velocity
S_{q-1}v
, which satisfies an integral equation of type

\Psi_{q}(t,x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}S_{q-1}v(\tau,\Psi_{q}(\tau,x))d\tau.

Thanks to the Proposition
2.1
, we write

\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla S_{q-1}v(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}d\tau\leqslant C(q+2)\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\triangleq V_{q}(t).

Thus, we can deduce from straightforward computations that the flow
\Psi^{1}_{q}\triangleq\Psi_{q}
and its inverse
\Psi^{-1}_{q}
satisfy

\|\nabla\Psi_{q}^{\pm 1}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant e^{V_{q}(t)}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\|\nabla^{2}\Psi_{q}^{\pm 1}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C2^{q}V_{q}(t)e^{V_{q}(t)},

(2.27)

Let us consider the functions

\bar{f}_{q}(t,x)=f_{q}(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x)),\quad\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}(t,x)=\mathtt{F}_{q}(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x))\;\;\textnormal{and}\;\;\bar{\bf R}_{q}={\bf R}_{q}(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x)).

Then from direct computations we infer

\displaystyle\Delta f_{q}(t)\circ\Psi_{q}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla\bar{f}(t,x)\cdot(\Delta\Psi_{q}^{-1})(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x))

(2.28)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Big\langle\nabla^{2}\bar{f}(t,x)\cdot(\partial_{i}\Psi_{q}^{-1})(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x)),(\partial_{i}\Psi_{q}^{-1})(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x))\big\rangle.

In addition, differentiating the integral equation of
\Psi_{q}^{-1}
leads after straightforward computations to the following result

(\partial_{i}\Psi_{q}^{-1})(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x))=e_{i}+g_{q}^{i}(t,x),

(2.29)

where the functions
g^{i}_{q}
satisfy

\|g_{q}^{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant V_{q}(t)e^{V_{q}(t)}\triangleq g_{q}(t).

Collecting (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) allows to check that
\bar{f}_{q}
satisfies the following parabolic equation

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}-\kappa\Delta\big)\bar{f}_{q}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\sum_{i=1}^{d}\big\langle\nabla^{2}\bar{f}_{q}(t,x)g^{i}_{q}(t,x),g^{i}_{q}(t,x)\big\rangle+2\kappa\sum_{i=1}^{d}\big\langle\nabla^{2}\bar{f}_{q}(t,x)e_{i},g^{i}_{q}(t,x)\big\rangle

\displaystyle+\kappa\nabla\bar{f}_{q}(t,x)\cdot(\Delta\Psi_{q}^{-1})(t,\Psi_{q}(t,x))+\bar{\bf R}_{q}+\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}(t,x).

One of the main difficulties that we should face is that the frequency spectrum of
\bar{f}_{q}
is not necessarily localized in a compact set. To deal with this, we shall first localize it through the operators
\Delta_{j}
, with
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, classical smoothing effects (see for instance
[
4
, Lemma 2.4]
) of the heat semigroup allow to get

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant Ce^{-c\kappa t2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}f_{q}(0)\|_{L^{p}}+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\bf R}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle+C\kappa(g_{q}+g_{q}^{2}(t))\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\nabla^{2}\bar{f}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle+C\kappa 2^{q}g_{q}(t)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\nabla\bar{f}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau.

To bound the second term of the r.h.s. of the above estimate, Bernstein’s inequality helps to write

\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\bf R}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant C2^{-j}\|\nabla({\bf R}_{q}\circ\Psi_{q}(t))\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant C2^{q-j}\|\nabla\Psi_{q}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|{\bf R}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}.

Consequently, the combination of the last two estimates, the condition (
2.25
) and Lemma
A.3
applied with
s=0

enable to have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle Ce^{-c\kappa t2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}(0)\|_{L^{p}}+(q+2)2^{q-j}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}e^{V_{q}(\tau)}\|f(\tau)\|_{B^{0}_{p,\infty}}d\tau

\displaystyle+C\kappa\big(g_{q}(t)+g_{q}^{2}(t)\big)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\nabla^{2}\bar{f}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle+C\kappa 2^{q}g_{q}(t)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\nabla\bar{f}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau.

Now, owing to the fact

\|f(\tau)\|_{B^{0}_{p,\infty}}\leqslant\|f(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant G(t),\quad\forall\tau\leqslant t,

we obtain

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant Ce^{-c\kappa t2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}(0)\|_{L^{p}}+(q+2)2^{q-j}G(t)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}e^{V_{q}(\tau)}d\tau

\displaystyle+C\kappa\big(g_{q}(t)+g_{q}^{2}(t)\big)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\nabla^{2}\bar{f}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau

\displaystyle+C\kappa 2^{q}g_{q}(t)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\nabla\bar{f}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{-c\kappa(t-\tau)2^{2j}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}d\tau.

(2.30)

Taking the
L^{r}_{t}-
norm in both sides of (
2.4
) and employing Young’s inequality we obtain, due to (
2.25
) and the time monotonicity of
g_{q}

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}\leqslant

\displaystyle C(\kappa 2^{2j})^{-\frac{1}{r}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}(0)\|_{L^{p}}+C2^{q-j}G(t)(\kappa 2^{2j})^{-\frac{1}{r}}\big(e^{V_{q}(t)}-1\big)

\displaystyle+C\big(g_{q}(t)+g_{q}^{2}(t)\big)2^{-2j}\|\nabla^{2}\bar{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}+Cg_{q}(t)2^{q}2^{-2j}\|\nabla\bar{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}

\displaystyle+C(\kappa 2^{2j})^{-\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}_{t}L^{p}},

(2.31)

for all
m\in[1,r]
. To estimate
\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}_{t}L^{p}}
we use the boundedness of
\Delta_{j}
together with the fact that
\Psi_{q}
preserves Lebesgue measure,

\|\Delta_{j}\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant\|\bar{\mathtt{F}}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant\|\mathtt{F}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}.

By making use of (
2.27
) and applying once again the invariance of Lebesgue measure by the flow
\Psi_{q}
, one deduces

\|\nabla\bar{f}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant C2^{q}e^{V_{q}(t)}\|f_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}

(2.32)

and

\|\nabla^{2}\bar{f}_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant C2^{2q}e^{V_{q}(t)}\|f_{q}(t)\|_{L^{p}}.

(2.33)

Hence, plugging these estimates into (
2.4
) and summing over
j\geqslant q-N_{0}
, we end up with

\displaystyle(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\sum_{j\geqslant q-N_{0}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}\leqslant

\displaystyle 2^{\frac{2N_{0}}{r}}\|f_{q}(0)\|_{L^{p}}+2^{2N_{0}}g_{q}(t)(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|f_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}

(2.34)

\displaystyle+C2^{N_{0}(1+\frac{2}{r})}G(t)\big(e^{V_{q}(t)}-1\big)+2^{2N_{0}(\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}})}(\kappa 2^{2q})^{-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}}\|{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}_{t}L^{p}}.

Concerning the lower frequencies, we exploit Lemma
A.2
in order to get

\sum_{j\leqslant q-N_{0}}\|\Delta_{j}\bar{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}\leqslant C2^{-N_{0}}e^{V_{q}(t)}\|f_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}.

(2.35)

Putting together (
2.34
), (
2.35
) and the estimate

\|f_{q}(0)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{p}}\leqslant G(t),

yields in view of
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\leqslant 1

\displaystyle(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|f_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{{2N_{0}}}G(t)

\displaystyle+2^{2N_{0}}g_{q}(t)(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|f_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}

(2.36)

\displaystyle+C2^{-N_{0}}e^{V_{q}(t)}(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|f_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}

\displaystyle+C2^{3N_{0}}G(t)\big(e^{V_{q}(t)}-1\big)

\displaystyle+2^{{2N_{0}}}(\kappa 2^{2q})^{-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}}\|{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}_{t}L^{p}}.

Accordingly, if we impose to
t
and
N_{0}
the following two conditions

2^{2N_{0}}g_{q}(t)\leqslant\frac{1}{4}\quad\mbox{and}\quad C2^{-N_{0}}e^{V_{q}(t)}\leqslant\frac{1}{4},

(2.37)

then we will have for every
q\geqslant N_{0}

(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|f_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}\leqslant C2^{4N_{0}}\big(G(t)+(\kappa 2^{2q})^{-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}}\|{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}_{t}L^{p}}\big).

(2.38)

In fact, the conditions given (
2.37
) are satisfied as soos as one takes

(q+2)\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\leqslant C_{0}.

(2.39)

The constant
C_{0}
is absolute, and, a fortiori
N_{0}
, whose choice does not depend on
q
. To be clear, we first take
V_{q}(t)\leqslant 1
and we select
N_{0}
so that
2^{-N_{0}}\leqslant\frac{1}{4eC}
, then we to get the first condition in (
2.37
) it suffices to take
V_{q}(t)
small enough such that
C2^{2N_{0}}g_{q}(t)\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
. This latter point is feasible because the function
xe^{x}
tends to zero when
x
approaches zero. Finally, we establish the existence of a fixed integer
N_{0}
and a small constant
C_{0}
such that, for any
t
satisfying (
2.39
) and for any
q\geqslant N_{0}
we have

(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}\leqslant C\big(G(t)+(\kappa 2^{2q})^{-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}}\|{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}_{t}L^{p}}\big).

In what follows, we will remove the smallness condition (
2.39
) and extend the above estimate for any arbitrary time
t
. For this aim, we take any integer
q\geqslant N_{0}
and split the time interval
[0,t]
in the following way

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{N}=t,\quad(q+2)\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\approx C_{0},\quad\forall i\in\{1,2,..,N-1\}.

Notice that by summing up the last condition, we find the estimate

N\leqslant C\Big(1+(q+2)\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\Big),

Now, reproducing the previous analysis in the interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
with the suitable adaptations in particular for the flow estimates, we succeed to establish

(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}([t_{i},t_{i+1}],L^{p})}\leqslant C\big(G(t_{i})+(\kappa 2^{2q})^{-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}}\|{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}([t_{i},t_{i+1}];L^{p})}\big).

Therefore, since
G
is increasing we get for any
q\geqslant N_{0}

(\kappa 2^{2q})\|{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}^{r}\leqslant C^{r}N\left(G(t)\right)^{r}+C^{r}(\kappa 2^{2q})^{-\frac{r}{m^{\prime}}}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\|{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}([t_{i},t_{i+1}];L^{p})}^{r}.

Remind that
m\leqslant r
and then by the embedding
\ell^{1}\hookrightarrow\ell^{\frac{r}{m}}
we infer

(\kappa 2^{2q})\|{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}^{r}\leqslant C^{r}N\left(G(t)\right)^{r}+C^{r}(\kappa 2^{2q})^{-\frac{r}{m^{\prime}}}\|{\mathtt{F}}_{q}\|_{L^{m}([0,t];L^{p})}^{r}.

(2.40)

Concerning the lower frequencies
q\leqslant N_{0}
, we write

(\kappa 2^{2q})^{\frac{1}{r}}\|{f}_{q}\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{p}}\leqslant C(\kappa t)^{\frac{1}{r}}G(t).

(2.41)

Finally, combining (
2.40
) and (
2.41
) we reach the desired estimate.
∎

An important application of the above proposition is the establishment of maximal smoothing effects for transport-diffusion equations drifted by a log-Lipschitz vector field. Consider a solution of the equation.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\rho+v\cdot\nabla\rho-\kappa\Delta\rho=0,\\
\rho_{|_{t=0}}=\rho_{0}.\end{array}\right.

(2.42)

then the following result holds.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
T>0
and
v
be a divergence-free vector field belonging to
L^{1}([0,T];LL)
whose vorticity satisfies
\omega\in L^{1}([0,T];L^{\infty})
. If
\rho
is a solution of (
2.42
) such that

\|\nabla\rho(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant G(t),\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(2.43)

for some increasing function
G
, then, for any
q\geqslant-1
,
i\in\{1,2\}
and
t\in[0,T]
, there holds that

\displaystyle\kappa 2^{2q}\|\Delta_{q}(\partial_{i}\rho)\|_{L_{t}^{1}L^{\infty}}\lesssim G(t)\Big(1+\kappa t+(q+2)\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\Big).

(2.44)

Proof.

Applying the partial derivative
\partial_{i}
to (
2.42
) yields

\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\rho+v\cdot\nabla\partial_{i}\rho-\kappa\Delta\partial_{i}\rho=\partial_{i}v\cdot\nabla\rho.

(2.45)

Hence, by applying Proposition
2.5
with
f\equiv\partial_{i}\rho
and
\mathtt{F}\equiv\partial_{i}v\cdot\nabla\rho
yields

\kappa 2^{2q}\|\Delta_{q}(\partial_{i}\rho)\|_{L_{t}^{1}L^{\infty}}\lesssim G(t)\Big(1+\kappa t+(q+2)\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\Big)+\|\Delta_{q}(\partial_{i}v\cdot\nabla\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}.

(2.46)
