It remains to estimate the term
\|\Delta_{q}(\partial_{i}v\cdot\nabla\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}
. By virtue of Bony’s decomposition, we write

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{q}(\partial_{i}v\cdot\nabla\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{|j-q|\leq 4}\|\Delta_{q}\big(S_{j-1}\partial_{i}v\cdot\Delta_{j}\nabla\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}+\sum_{|j-q|\leq 4}\|\Delta_{q}\big(\Delta_{j}\partial_{i}v\cdot S_{j-1}\nabla\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{{j\geqslant q-4}}\|\Delta_{q}\big(\widetilde{\Delta}_{j}\partial_{i}v\cdot\Delta_{j}\nabla\rho\big)\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{II}_{1}+\textnormal{II}_{2}+\textnormal{II}_{3}.

We treat each term separately. For
\textnormal{II}_{1}
, Proposition
2.1
ensures that

\displaystyle\textnormal{II}_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{|j-q|\leq 4}\|S_{j-1}\partial_{i}v\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C(q+2)\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau.

To estimate the term
\textnormal{II}_{2}
, we use again Proposition
2.1
leading to

\displaystyle\textnormal{II}_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{|j-q|\leq 4}\|S_{j-1}\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla v\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C(q+2)\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau.

Finally, in order to estimate the remainder term
\textnormal{II}_{3}
, we need to use the divergence-free condition on
v
to obtain

\displaystyle\textnormal{II}_{3}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C2^{q}\sum_{j\geqslant q-4}\|\widetilde{\Delta}_{j}\nabla v\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}}\|{\Delta}_{j}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\sum_{j\geqslant q-4}(j+2)2^{q-j}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\Big((q+2)\sum_{j\geqslant q-4}2^{q-j}+\sum_{j\geqslant q-4}(j-q)2^{q-j}\Big).

Now, using the fact that

\sup_{q\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{j\geqslant q-4}2^{q-j}+\sum_{j\geqslant q-4}(j-q)2^{q-j}<\infty

yields

\textnormal{II}_{3}\leqslant C(q+2)\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau.

Consequently, plugging the preceding estimates into (
2.46
) and using the assumption (
2.43
) achieves the proof of Corollary
2.3
.
∎

3.
Inviscid case

This section is devoted to the study of the inviscid Boussinesq system written in the vorticity-density reformulation as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\omega+v\cdot\nabla\omega=\partial_{1}\rho,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},&\\
\partial_{t}\rho+v\cdot\nabla\rho=0,&\\
v=\nabla^{\perp}\Delta^{-1}\omega,&\\
(\omega,\rho)|_{t=0}=(\omega_{0},\rho_{0}).\end{array}\right.

(3.1)

We shall state a local well-posedness result for (
3.1
) with general initial data covering the result of Theorem
1.1
. Thereby, we show first how we deduce the proof of Theorem
1.1
from the general statement of Theorem
3.1
below. Afterward, we shall move to the proof of the general version. We recall that all the notation and definitions used in this section are borrowed from Section
2
.

3.1.
Generalized singular patches

We intend to describe a full statement on the well-posedness issue for (
3.1
) with a general framework covering a large class containing in particular singular patches. Our main result reads as follows.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
(s,m,a)\in(0,1)^{2}\times(1,\infty)
and let
\Sigma_{0}
be a compact negligible subset of the plane. Consider a divergence-free vector field
v_{0}
with vorticity
\omega_{0}
in
L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}
and let
\rho_{0}
be a real-valued function in
W^{1,a}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap C^{1+m}((\Sigma_{0})_{r})
,
2
2

2

We recall that the definition of an
h
-enlargement of a set
F
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is given by (
2.1
).
for some
r>0
, such that
\nabla\rho_{0}
vanishes on
\Sigma_{0}.
Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}=(X_{0,\lambda,h})_{(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}]}
be a family of vector fields of class
C^{s}
as well as their divergences and suppose that this family is
\Sigma_{0}
-admissible of order
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
such that

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\|\omega_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0,h})}^{s}+\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\|\rho_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0,h})}^{s+1}<\infty.

Then, there exists
T>0
such that the Boussinesq system (
3.1
) admits a unique solution

(\omega,\rho)\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T],L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}\big)\times L^{\infty}\big([0,T],W^{1,a}\cap W^{1,\infty}\big).

In addition, we have

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}\frac{\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{h}^{c})}}{-\log h}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]),

where
\Sigma_{t}=\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0})
and
\Psi
is the flow associated to
v
. Moreover, there exists
C_{0,T}>0
, depending only on the initial data and
T
, and there exists a triplet
\Xi_{1}=(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})
of non-negative numbers, such that

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]\atop t\in[0,T]}h^{\beta_{1}}\|\omega(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{h^{\alpha_{1}}},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s}+\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta_{1}}\|\rho(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{h^{\alpha_{1}}},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s+1}\leqslant C_{0,T},

(3.2)

where the transported
\mathcal{X}_{t}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
by the flow
\Psi
, defined by

X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,\cdot))\triangleq\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi(t,\cdot),

is
\Sigma_{t}
–admissible of order
\Xi_{1}
and belongs to
L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{s})
, for all
(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}]
.

Furthermore, there exists a continuous non-increasing function
z:[0,T]\longrightarrow(0,m]
such that for
any
p\in[a,\infty]
,
we have

\forall t\in[0,T],\quad\|(d(\cdot,\Sigma_{t}))^{-z(t)}\nabla\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}<\infty,

(3.3)

Remark 3.1
.

Overall, the main improvement in Theorem
1.1
compared to the result of
[
39
]
is the following: The initial density is no longer required to be constant in a neighborhood of the singular set
\Sigma_{0}
as assumed in
[
39
]
. This technical assumption is replaced here by a weaker compatibility assumption:
\rho_{0}\in C^{1+m}((\Sigma_{0})_{r})
and
\nabla\rho_{0}
should vanish on the singular set
\Sigma_{0}
. It is not at all clear whether the LWP issue can be performed without the compatibility assumption and we expect some instability behavior to occur.

Remark 3.2
.

In Theorem
3.1
, we assume that
\rho_{0}
is of class
C^{1+m}
in a small neighborhood of the singular set
\Sigma_{0}
. This assumption can be relaxed for the a priori estimates as stated in
Proposition
3.1
. However, we still need the Hölder regularity in the construction of the solutions by smoothing the initial data that should obey the compatibility assumption. Notice that the persistence of
C^{1+m}-
regularity is out of reach due to the low regularity of the velocity field.

Let us now show how to deduce the proof for Theorem
1.1
from the result of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The idea is the same as in
[
39
]
. For the convenience of the reader, we briefly outline the proof. By assumption, we may write

\partial\Omega_{0}=\big\{x\in V:f_{0}(x)=0\big\},

for some
V
, a neighborhood of
\partial\Omega_{0}
, and for some function
f_{0}
in
C^{1+s}
satisfying

|\nabla f_{0}(x)|\geqslant Cd(x,\Sigma_{0})^{\gamma}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\gamma>0.

Let
(\theta_{h})_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}
be a family of smooth functions with
\theta_{h}\equiv 1
in
(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
and supported in
(\Sigma_{0})_{h^{\alpha}}^{c}

(where
\alpha>1
)
which satisfies, for any
\widetilde{s}>0

\|\theta_{h}\|_{C^{\widetilde{s}}}\leqslant C_{\widetilde{s}}h^{{-\widetilde{s}}}.

Given a function
\widetilde{\theta}
in
C^{\infty}
, with
\widetilde{\theta}\equiv 1
in
V
, and setting

X_{0,0,h}=\nabla^{\perp}(\theta_{h}f_{0}),\quad X_{0,1,h}=\theta_{h}(1-\widetilde{\theta})\vec{e}_{1}.

It is easy to show that
\mathcal{X}_{0}=(X_{0,j,h})_{(j,h)\in\{0,1\}\times(0,e^{-1}]}
is of class
C^{s}
and
\Sigma_{0}
–admissible of some order
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
, see, for instance
[
39
]
. Additionally, we can check that

\partial_{X_{0,j,h}}\omega_{0}=0,\quad\forall j\in\{1,2\},

and the assumption
\rho_{0}\in C^{1+s}
in Theorem
1.1
implies

\partial_{X_{0,j,h}}\rho_{0}\in C^{s},\quad\forall j\in\{1,2\}.

More precisely, we have

h^{\beta}\frac{\|\partial_{X_{0,j,h}}\rho_{0}\|_{C^{s}}}{I(\Sigma_{h},X_{0,j,h})}\leqslant h^{\beta}\frac{\|X_{0,j,h}\|_{C^{s}}}{I(\Sigma_{h},X_{0,j,h})}\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{s+1}}\leqslant\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}N_{s}(\Sigma_{h},\mathcal{X}_{h})\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{s+1}}.

Consequently, we obtain

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\|\omega_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0,h})}^{s}+\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\|\rho_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0,h})}^{s+1}<\infty.

Finally, the assumption
\rho_{0}\in C^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in Theorem
1.1
insures then that
\rho_{0}\in C^{1+m}((\Sigma_{0})_{r})
for the particular choice
m=s.

Therefore, the hypotheses of Theorem
3.1
are satisfied and the local well-posedness in
Theorem
1.1
follows.
Moreover, Theorem
3.1
gives

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}\frac{\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{h}^{c})}}{-\log h}\in L^{\infty}([0,T])

and for all
h\in(0,e^{-1}]
,

X_{t,0,h}(\Psi(t,\cdot))\triangleq\partial_{X_{0,0,h}}\Psi(t,\cdot)\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{s}).

(3.4)

Finally, concerning the persistence regularity of the boundary of the initial vortex patch, the arguments are the same as in
[
17
, Chapter 9]
. For the sake of completeness we shall recall the main lines of the proof. Take
x_{0}\in\partial\Omega_{0}\textbackslash\Sigma_{0}
and consider
\gamma^{0}
as the
C^{s+1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
-parametrization of the connected component of
\partial\Omega_{0}\textbackslash\Sigma_{0}
containing
x_{0}

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{0}(\tau)=X_{0,0,h}(\gamma^{0}(\tau)),\\
\gamma^{0}(0)=x_{0}.\end{array}\right.

Thus the connected component of
\partial\Omega_{t}\textbackslash\Sigma_{t}
containing
\psi(t,x_{0})
is parametrized by

\gamma_{t}(\tau)\triangleq\Psi(t,\gamma^{0}(\tau)),\quad\forall\tau\in\mathbb{R}

which satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{\tau}\gamma_{t}(\tau)=\partial_{X_{0,0,h}}\Psi(t,\gamma^{0}(\tau)),\\
\gamma_{t}(0)=\Psi(t,x_{0}).\end{array}\right.

Hence, according to (
3.4
), we infer that
\gamma_{t}\in C^{1+s}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\partial_{\tau}\gamma_{t}(\tau)
is not vanishing provided that
h
is small enough.
We finally deduce that
\gamma_{t}
is a local parametrization of the connected component of
\Psi(t,\partial\Omega_{0}\textbackslash\Sigma_{0})
containing
\Psi(t,x_{0})
.
∎

The rest of this section will be dedicated to the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

3.2.
A priori estimates

Since we are interested in the solutions generated from initial data belonging to Yudovich class, the major issue to prove Theorem
3.1
arises from the difficulty of the propagation of the
L^{p}
-norms of
\nabla\rho
and
\omega
. The key observation to do is the following. The quantity
\nabla\rho
obeys the forcing transport equation

(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)\nabla\rho=-\nabla v\cdot\nabla\rho.

(3.5)

Then the control of the Lipschitz norm of
v
seems to be relevant to propagate the
L^{p}
-norm of
\nabla\rho.
Unfortunately, this information on the velocity field is not guaranteed if the vorticity
\omega
is only bounded. In
[
39
]
, the authors impose to the initial density to be constant on a small neighborhood of the singular set, and in view of the transport structure, the density
\rho(t)
remains constant in a neighborhood of the singular set
\Sigma_{t}
. This allows to control the source term in (
3.5
) and deduce
L^{p}
-estimates for
\nabla\rho.
Our goal here is to relax the compatibility assumption in
[
39
]
and replace it by a platitude assumption. The precise statement is discussed below and constitutes the cornerstone of the a priori estimates that we shall establish later.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\Sigma_{0}
be a compact negligible set in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
(m,p)\in(0,1)\times[1,\infty)
. Let
\rho_{0}
be a scalar function satisfying

\nabla\rho_{0}\in L^{p},\text{ and }\quad\sup_{x\notin\Sigma_{0}}{|\varphi_{0}(x)|}^{-m}|\nabla\rho_{0}(x)|<\infty,

(3.6)

where
\varphi_{0}
is given by (
2.4
). Let
(\omega,\rho)
be any smooth maximal solution to (
3.1
) defined on
[0,T^{\star})
and let the function
m(t)
be given by

m(t)\triangleq m-\int_{0}^{t}\|{v}(\tau)\|_{L(\Sigma_{\tau})}e^{\int_{0}^{\tau}\|v(s)\|_{LL}ds}d\tau,\quad\forall t\in[0,T^{\star}).

(3.7)

There exists
C_{0}>0
depending only on
\rho_{0}
and
\Sigma_{0}
such that the following holds.
If
m(T)\geqslant 0
, for some
T<T^{\star}
, then for all
t\in[0,T]
and for all
r\in[p,\infty]
, we have that

\|\nabla\rho(t)\|_{L^{r}}\leqslant C_{0}

and

\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{r}}\leqslant\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{r}}+C_{0}t.

Furthermore, let
\Psi
be the flow associated to
v
and set

\varphi_{t}(x)\triangleq\varphi_{0}(\Psi^{-1}(t,x)),

(3.8)

then, for all
t\in[0,T]
and for all
r\in[p,\infty]
, we have

\|\varphi^{-m(t)}_{t}\nabla\rho(t)\|_{L^{r}}\leqslant C_{0}.

Proof.

Let
\beta
be the function given by
\beta(t)=2m(t)
and introduce

\varrho(t,x)=\big[\varphi_{t}(x)\big]^{-\beta(t)}|\nabla\rho(t,x)|^{2},

where
\varphi_{t}
is given by (
3.8
). One can check that

\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)\big[\varphi_{t}(x)\big]^{-\beta(t)}=-\beta^{\prime}(t)\log\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)\big[\varphi_{t}(x)\big]^{-\beta(t)}.

(3.9)

We notice that from the definition of
L(\Sigma_{t})
stated in (
2.2
), one deduces

|\nabla{v}(t,x)|\leqslant\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t})),\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

On the other hand, from the equation of
\rho
, we have

\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)(\partial_{i}\rho)^{2}=-2(\partial_{i}{v})\cdot\nabla\rho\,\partial_{i}\rho.

Thus, we may write
3
3

3

We recall that the modulus of a
2
d vector is given by
|v|=\sqrt{v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}}.

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)|\nabla\rho|^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\partial_{i}{v})\cdot\nabla\rho\,\partial_{i}\rho

\displaystyle\leqslant 2|\nabla{v}||\nabla\rho|^{2}.

Hence,
|\nabla\rho|^{2}
satisfies the following system on
\theta

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\theta\leqslant 2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\theta\\
\theta_{|_{t=0}}=|\nabla\rho_{0}|^{2}.\end{array}\right.

(3.10)

Therefore, combining (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) for
\theta=|\nabla\rho|^{2}
, gives

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\varrho

\displaystyle\leqslant\big(-\beta^{\prime}(t)\ln\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)+2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t})\big)\varrho.

(3.11)

Since we have, from (
3.8
) and (
2.3
), that

\ln\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)=-\ln^{+}\big[d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)\big],

then, inserting this identity into (
3.11
) yields

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\varrho

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(\beta^{\prime}(t)\ln^{+}\big[d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)\big]+2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\Big)\varrho.

(3.12)

Using the inequality of Lemma
2.7
we infer

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\varrho

\displaystyle\leqslant\ln^{+}\big[d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)\big]\Big(\beta^{\prime}(t)+2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}\Big)\varrho.

(3.13)

At this stage we shall impose the conditions

\beta^{\prime}(t)+2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}\leqslant 0\quad\hbox{with}\quad\beta(0)=2m,

which are satisfied with the choice

\beta(t)=2m-2\int_{0}^{t}\|{v}(\tau)\|_{L(\Sigma_{\tau})}e^{\int_{0}^{\tau}\|v(s)\|_{LL}ds}d\tau,

and equivalently

m(t)=m-\int_{0}^{t}\|{v}(\tau)\|_{L(\Sigma_{\tau})}e^{\int_{0}^{\tau}\|v(s)\|_{LL}ds}d\tau.

With this choice of
m(t)
, we obtain

\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\varrho\leqslant 0,

(3.14)

and consequently, we get for all
x\in\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0}^{c})

{[\varphi_{t}(x)]}^{-2m(t)}|\nabla\rho(t,x)|^{2}\leqslant\sup_{y\notin\Sigma_{0}}{[\varphi_{0}(y)]}^{-2m}|\nabla\rho_{0}(y)|^{2}\triangleq(C_{\infty})^{2}.

(3.15)

Therefore, the fact that
\Sigma_{0}
is a negligible set implies that
\Sigma_{0}^{c}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Hence, the fact that the flow
\Psi
is a homeomorphism yields that
\Psi(t,\Sigma_{0}^{c})
is also dense in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It follows that (
3.15
) holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Thereafter, we find that

|\nabla\rho(t,x)|^{2}\leqslant C_{\infty}^{2}{[\varphi_{t}(x)]}^{2m(t)},\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

This implies in particular that

m(t)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow\|\nabla\rho(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C_{\infty}e^{-m(t)}\leqslant C_{\infty}.

Similarly, we obtain from (
3.14
)

\|(\varphi_{t}(\cdot)\big)^{-m(t)}\nabla\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant C_{p},

(3.16)

where

C_{p}\triangleq\|\varphi_{0}^{-m}\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{p}}.

Remark that
C_{p}
is finite due to the assumption (
3.6
) and the fact that
\Sigma_{0}
is compact. Indeed, we can write in view of notation (
2.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\varphi_{0}(x)^{-m}\nabla\rho_{0}(x)\right|^{p}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(\Sigma_{0})_{e^{-1}}}\varphi_{0}(x)^{-m}\nabla\rho_{0}(x)dx+\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{p}((\Sigma_{0})_{e^{-1}}^{c})}^{p}

(3.17)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left|(\Sigma_{0})_{e^{-1}}\right|C_{\infty}+\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}.

On the other hand, we have

m(t)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow|\nabla\rho(t,\cdot)|=|\nabla\rho(t,x)|[\varphi_{t}(x)\big]^{-m(t)}[\varphi_{t}(x)\big]^{m(t)}\leqslant|\nabla\rho(t,x)|[\varphi_{t}(x)\big]^{-m(t)}.

Therefore, we obtain for any
r\in[p,\infty]

m(t)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow\|\nabla\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{r}}\leqslant C_{r}.

(3.18)

Using this estimate together with the vorticity equation in (
3.1
) give for all
r\in[p,\infty]

\displaystyle\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{r}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{r}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{r}}d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{r}}+C_{r}t,

This achieves the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Now we shall discuss the following corollary about a useful blow up criterion.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
(v,\rho)\in C\big([0,T^{\star}),C^{1+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
be a maximal solution to (
3.1
) satisfying the assumptions of Proposition
3.1
. Then

T^{\star}<\infty\Longrightarrow\lim_{t\to T^{\star}}m(t)<0,

where the function
m
is defined in (
3.7
).

Proof.

Assume that
T^{\star}<\infty
, then according to the blow up criterion proved in
[
16
]
, we have

\int_{0}^{T^{\star}}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}d\tau=\infty.

(3.19)

Now since
t\in[0,T^{\star})\mapsto m(t)
is non-increasing then
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to T^{\star}}m(t)\triangleq\ell}
exists and belongs to
[-\infty,\infty)
. At this level we distinguish two cases. The first one is when
\ell<0
and in this case the result is proved. However in the second case
\ell\geqslant 0
we get by the monotonicity that

\forall t\in[0,T^{\star}),\quad m(t)\geqslant 0.

Applying Proposition
3.1
we find

\|\nabla\rho(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C_{0}

which implies that

\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}d\tau\leqslant C_{0}t\leqslant C_{0}T^{\star}.

This contradicts (
3.19
).
∎

In the sequel, we shall explore some applications of Proposition
3.1
. The first one deals with the persistence of anisotropic regularity of
\omega
and
\rho
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
(s,a)\in(0,1)\times[1,\infty)
and
\Sigma_{0}
be a compact negligible set of the plane. Let
X_{0}
be a vector field of class
C^{s}
as well as its divergence and whose support is embedded in
(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
, for some
h\in(0,e^{-1}]
. Let
\rho_{0}
be a scalar function satisfying the assumptions of Proposition
3.1
. Consider a smooth maximal solution
(\omega,\rho)
of the system (
3.1
) defined on
[0,T^{\star})
, with the initial data
(\omega_{0},\rho_{0})
and denote by
X_{t}
the solution of the transport equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)X_{t}=\partial_{X_{t}}v.&\\
X_{|t=0}=X_{0}.\end{array}\right.

Then, the following assertions hold

\textnormal{supp}X_{t}\subset\big(\Sigma_{t}\big)_{\delta_{t}(h)}^{c}

and

\|\textnormal{div }X_{t}\|_{s}\leqslant\|\textnormal{div }X_{0}\|_{s}h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau},\quad t\in[0,T^{\star}),

where,
\delta_{t}(h)
is defined in (
2.8
).
Furthermore, let
T\in(0,T^{\star})
such that
m(T)\geqslant 0
,
with
m
being the function defined in (
3.7
).
Then we get for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\|}X_{t}\|_{s}+\|\partial_{X_{t}}\omega(t)\|_{s-1}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle Ce^{C_{0}t}\Big(\widetilde{\|}X_{0}\|_{s}+\|\partial_{X_{0}}\omega_{0}\|_{s-1}+\|\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}\Big)h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau},

\|\partial_{X_{t}}\rho(t)\|_{s}\leqslant\|\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau},

where
C>0
is a constant independent of the initial data and
W(t)
is given by (
2.12
).

Proof.

The results concerning the support of
X_{t}
were already proved in Corollary
2.2
. Moreover, by applying Corollary
2.2
for
p=\infty
it happens

\displaystyle\widetilde{\|}X_{t}\|_{s}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}\Big(\widetilde{\|}X_{0}\|_{s}+C\int_{0}^{t}h^{C\int_{0}^{\tau}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\omega(\tau)\|_{s-1}d\tau\Big),

(3.20)

Now, we focus on the estimate of
\partial_{X_{t}}\omega.
Since
\partial_{X_{t}}
commutes with the transport, that is

[\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla,\partial_{X_{t}}]=0,

(3.21)

then we deduce from the vorticity equation that

\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)\partial_{X_{t}}\omega=\partial_{X_{t}}\partial_{1}\rho.

Applying Proposition
2.2
allows to find

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{t}}\omega(t)\|_{s-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{0}}\omega_{0}\|_{s-1}h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\partial_{1}\rho(\tau)\|_{s-1}h^{-C\int_{\tau}^{t}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{0}}\omega_{0}\|_{s-1}h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\rho(\tau)\|_{s}h^{-C\int_{\tau}^{t}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}d\tau

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\widetilde{\|}X_{\tau}\|_{s}h^{-C\int_{\tau}^{t}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}d\tau,

where, we have used the following inequalities

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\partial_{1}\rho(\tau)\|_{s-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\rho(\tau)\|_{s}+\|(\partial_{1}X_{\tau})\cdot\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{s-1}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\rho(\tau)\|_{s}+\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\widetilde{\|}X_{\tau}\|_{s}

and the estimate of the last term follows from Corollary
2.1
. Now, due to (
3.21
), we have that

(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)\partial_{X_{t}}\rho(t)=0.

It is easy to check that
\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}
is supported in
(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
since
\text{supp }X_{0}\subset(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
. Thus, Proposition
2.2
gives

\|\partial_{X_{\tau}}\rho(\tau)\|_{s}\leqslant\|\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}\,h^{-C\int_{0}^{\tau}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{t}}\omega(t)\|_{s-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\Big(\|\partial_{X_{0}}\omega_{0}\|_{s-1}+\|\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}t\Big)h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\widetilde{\|}X_{\tau}\|_{s}h^{-C\int_{\tau}^{t}W(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}}d\tau.

Putting together the preceding estimate with (
3.20
) we find that

\zeta(t)\lesssim\zeta(0)+\|\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}t+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}+1\Big)\zeta(\tau)d\tau,

where

\zeta(t)\triangleq\Big(\|\partial_{X_{t}}\omega(t)\|_{s-1}+\widetilde{\|}X_{t}\|_{s}\Big)h^{C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}.

Therefore, Gronwall’s lemma ensures that

\zeta(t)\leqslant\big(\zeta(0)+\|\partial_{X_{0}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}\big)e^{C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\rho(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}d\tau}e^{Ct}

(3.23)

and applying Proposition
3.1
completes the proof.
∎

Now, we are in a position to control the Lipschitz norm of the velocity outside the transported set of
\Sigma_{0}
by the flow. In addition to that, we shall get a lower bound of the function
m(t)
in terms of the initial data. This provides an a priori bound on the existence time of the solutions to the system (
3.1
).

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(s,a)\in(0,1)\times[1,\infty)
,
\Sigma_{0}
be a compact negligible set of the plane and
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\big(X_{0,\lambda,h}\big)_{\lambda\in\Lambda,h\in(0,e^{-1}]}
be a family of vector fields which is
\Sigma_{0}
-admissible of order
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
. Let
(\omega,\rho)
be a smooth maximal solution of the
system
(\ref{eqn:omega})
defined on a time interval
[0,T^{\star})
. We assume that
\omega_{0}\in L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}
, and that
\rho_{0}
satisfies the assumptions in Proposition
3.1
.
Assume in addition that

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\|\omega_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0,h})}^{s}+\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\|\rho_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0,h})}^{s+1}<\infty.

Then, there exists
T\in(0,T^{\star})
such that the estimates for
\rho
and
\omega
in Proposition
3.1
hold on
[0,T]
and

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\leqslant C_{0}.

Notice that
T
is defined as the unique solution to
\Gamma(T)=m
, where

\Gamma(t)\triangleq te^{\exp{(C_{0}(1+t)^{2})}}

(3.24)

and
C_{0}>0
is a constant depending only on the initial data and
\Sigma_{0}.

Furthermore, there exist some continuous functions
\Xi(t)=(\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t))
such that,

\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta(t)}\|\omega(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s}+\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta(t)}\|\rho(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s+1}\leqslant C_{0}.

Moreover, the transported family
\mathcal{X}_{t}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
by the flow
\Psi
defined by

X_{t,\lambda,h}(\cdot)\triangleq\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi(t,\Psi^{-1}(t,\cdot)),

is
\Sigma_{t}
–admissible of order
\Xi(t)
and belongs to
L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{s}),
for all
(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}].

Proof.

Let
T\in(0,T^{\star})
such that

m(T)\geqslant 0,

(3.25)

where
m(t)
was defined in Proposition
3.2
and takes the form

m(t)=m-\int_{0}^{t}\|{v}(\tau)\|_{L(\Sigma_{\tau})}e^{\int_{0}^{\tau}\|v(\tau^{\prime})\|_{LL}d\tau^{\prime}}d\tau.

(3.26)

Since
m
is non-increasing then we easily find

\forall t\in[0,T],\,m(t)\geqslant 0

This ensures in particular that all the a priori estimates developed in Proposition
3.1
and
Proposition
3.2
are valid on
[0,T]
.
Let us examine the estimate of

\Upsilon(t)\triangleq\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\widetilde{\|}X_{t,\lambda,h}\|_{s}+\|\partial_{X_{t,\lambda,h}}\omega(t)\|_{s-1}.

Then combining Proposition
3.1
and Proposition
3.2
yields for any
t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle\Upsilon(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{0}e^{C_{0}t}\big(1+\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}\big)\Big(\widetilde{\|}X_{0,\lambda,h}\|_{s}+\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\omega_{0}\|_{s-1}+\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\rho_{0}\|_{s-1}\Big)h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}.

Hence, we deduce in view of the Definition
2.5
and (
2.19
)

\displaystyle\Upsilon(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}e^{C_{0}t}\big(1+\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big)\Big(N_{s}\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}\big)+\|\omega_{0}\|^{s}_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}}+\|\rho_{0}\|^{s+1}_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}}\Big)

\displaystyle\times I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}\big)h^{-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}e^{C_{0}t}\big(1+\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big)\sup_{0<h\leqslant e^{-1}}h^{\beta}\Big(N_{s}((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h})+\|\omega_{0}\|^{s}_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}}+\|\rho_{0}\|^{s+1}_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}}\Big)

\displaystyle\times I\big((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})\big)h^{-\beta-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}.

Putting together this estimate with the norm definition (
2.10
) allows to get (with a suitable adjustment of
C_{0}
)

\displaystyle\|\omega(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}e^{C_{0}t}h^{-\beta-C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau}.

(3.27)

Consequently, we obtain

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta(t)}\|\omega(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s}\leqslant C_{0}e^{C_{0}t},

where

\beta(t)\triangleq\beta+C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau>0.

(3.28)

Similarly, repeating the same arguments yields

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta(t)}\|\rho(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s+1}+\sup_{\underset{\lambda\in\Lambda}{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}}h^{\beta(t)}\frac{\widetilde{\|}X_{t,\lambda,h}\|_{s}}{I\big((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})\big)}\leqslant C_{0}e^{C_{0}t}.

The next target is to establish a Lipschitz estimate for the velocity. For this purpose, we combine Theorem
2.1
with Proposition
3.1
and the monotonicity of the
map
x\mapsto x\log\big(e+\frac{a}{x}\big)
to get

\displaystyle\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c})}\leqslant C\Big(\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}}+tC_{\infty}\Big)\log\bigg(e+\frac{\|\omega\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h})}^{s}}{\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}}\bigg).

Therefore, we find according to the estimate (
3.27
),

\displaystyle\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c})}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}(1+t)\bigg(C_{0}(1+t)-\Big(\beta+C\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau\Big)\log h\bigg).

Since
1\leqslant-\log h
, then it follows by virtue of (
2.20
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\|\nabla v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}^{c})}}{-\log\delta_{t}^{-1}(h)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}(1+t)\bigg(1+t+\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau\bigg)e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}.

Thus we infer from (
2.2
)

\displaystyle\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}(1+t)\bigg(1+t+\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau\bigg)e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}.

Thereby, from the definition of the function
W
(
2.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad W(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}(1+t)\bigg(1+t+\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau\bigg)e^{2\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}.

On the other hand, from Lemma
2.2
and Proposition
3.1
, we get

\displaystyle\|v(t)\|_{LL}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}}

(3.29)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}(1+t).

Hence,

\displaystyle\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad W(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0}(1+t)^{2}e^{C_{0}(1+t)^{2}}+e^{C_{0}(1+t)^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau.

Applying Gronwall lemma implies

\displaystyle\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad W(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\exp\Big(e^{C_{0}(1+t)^{2}}\Big).

It follows that

\displaystyle\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau\leqslant\Gamma(t)\triangleq t\,e^{\exp{C_{0}(1+t)^{2}}}\leqslant\Gamma(T).

(3.30)

Consequently, from the definition of
m(t)
in (
3.26
) and the definition of
W
in (
2.12
), we infer that

m(T)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad m(t)\geqslant m-\int_{0}^{t}W(\tau)d\tau\geqslant m-\Gamma(T).

(3.31)

Now, from Corollary
3.1
combined with the continuity of
t\mapsto m(t)
we deduce the existence of
\widetilde{T}\in(0,T^{\star})
such that

m(\widetilde{T})=0.

Combined with (
3.31
) yield

m-\Gamma(\widetilde{T})\leqslant 0.

Next, by defining
T
such that,

\Gamma(T)=m,

(3.32)

we obtain the lower bound of the maximal time existence

T\leqslant\widetilde{T}<T^{\star}.

This ends the proof of the Proposition
3.3
. We observe that
T\in(0,1).

∎

We conclude this section by the following corollary which is useful in the proof of the regularity persistence of the transported vortex patch.

Corollary 3.2
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
3.3
, we have

t\longmapsto\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi(t,\cdot)=X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,\cdot))\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T];C^{s}\big),\quad\forall(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}].

More precisely, there exists a constant
C_{0,h,T}>0
, depending only on the initial data,
h
and
T,
such that

\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad\|X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,\cdot))\|_{C^{s}}\leqslant C_{0,h,T}.

(3.33)

Proof.

Remark first that, due to Proposition
3.2
, we have

\|X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,\cdot))\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|X_{t,\lambda,h}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|X_{t,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\leqslant C_{0,h,T}.

For Hölder estimates, we claim that for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
|x-x^{\prime}|\leqslant\tfrac{h}{2}

\big|X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x))-X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x^{\prime}))\big|\leqslant C_{0,h,T}|x-x^{\prime}|^{s}.

(3.34)

To prove it, we shall consider two cases.

❖ If
x,x^{\prime}\in(\Sigma_{0})_{h}
, then from
\textnormal{supp}X_{0,\lambda,h}\subset(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
we easily get

\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi(t,x)=X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x))=\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi(t,x^{\prime})=X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x^{\prime}))=0.

❖If
x,x^{\prime}\in(\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{h}{2}}^{c}
, then we proceed as follows. We recall first from Proposition
3.3
that

\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi(t,\Psi^{-1}(t,\cdot))=X_{t,\lambda,h}(\cdot)\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T];C^{s}\big),\quad\forall(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}].

This implies that, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}

\displaystyle\big|X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x))-X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x^{\prime}))\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|X_{t,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\big|\Psi(t,x)-\Psi(t,x^{\prime})\big|^{s}

(3.35)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|X_{t,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\big|x-x^{\prime}\big|^{s}\sup_{z\in[x,x^{\prime}]}\big|\nabla\Psi(t,z)\big|^{s}.

By assumption
|x-x^{\prime}|\leqslant\frac{h}{2}
and therefore

\sup_{z\in[x,x^{\prime}]}\big|\nabla\Psi(t,z)\big|\leqslant\|\nabla\Psi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{h}{2}}^{c})},

which implies in turn that

\big|X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x))-X_{t,\lambda,h}(\Psi(t,x^{\prime}))\big|\lesssim\|X_{t,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\big|x-x^{\prime}\big|^{s}\|\nabla\Psi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{h}{2}}^{c})}.

(3.36)

Next, we intend to estimate
\nabla\Psi
in the right hand side of the preceding inequality. To this end, differentiating the equation of the flow and using Gronwall’s lemma yields

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Psi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{h}{2}}^{c})}\leqslant\exp\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v(\tau,\Psi(\tau,\cdot))\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{h}{2}}^{c})}d\tau\Big).

Moreover, Lemma
2.6
gives

\Psi(t,(\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{h}{2}}^{c})\subset\big(\Sigma_{t}\big)_{\delta_{t}(\frac{h}{2})}^{c}.

Hence we deduce

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Psi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{h}{2}}^{c})}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{\tau})_{\delta_{\tau}(\frac{h}{2})}^{c})}d\tau\Big)

(3.37)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{t}\log(\delta_{\tau}(\tfrac{h}{2}))\|v(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L(\Sigma_{\tau})}d\tau\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle e^{C_{0,h}},

where we have use in the last inequality the following consequences of Proposition
3.1
and Proposition
3.3
, for all
t\leqslant T

\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}\leqslant\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\|\omega(\tau)\|_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}}\leqslant C_{0}(1+t),

\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\|v(\tau)\|_{L(\Sigma_{\tau})}\leqslant C_{0}.

and the fact that
T\leqslant 1
(due to (
3.32
)).
Finally, in order to prove (
3.34
), it is enough to implement (
3.37
) in (
3.36
).
∎

3.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

In the sequel we shall give the main details on the proof of
Theorem
3.1
.

➊ Approximated solutions.

To prove the existence of a solution, we need to implement some modifications in the proof given in
[
39
]
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
, let us smooth out the initial data and consider the solutions to the system,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial_{t}v_{n}+v_{n}\cdot\nabla v_{n}+\nabla p_{n}=\rho_{n}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},&\\
\partial_{t}\rho_{n}+v_{n}\cdot\nabla\rho_{n}=0,&\\
\textnormal{div }v_{n}=0,\\
v_{0,n}=S_{n}v_{0},\quad\rho_{0,n}=\widetilde{S}_{n}\rho_{0},\end{array}\right.

(3.38)

where
S_{n}
is the cut-off in frequency defined in Section 2 and
\widetilde{S}_{n}
is given by

\widetilde{S}_{n}\rho_{0}\triangleq\chi_{r}\rho_{0}+(1-\chi_{r})S_{n}\rho_{0},

with
\chi_{r}
is a smooth cut-off function with values in
[0,1]
and satisfying

\chi_{r}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{ if }d(x,\Sigma_{0})\leqslant\frac{r}{2},\\
0&\text{ if }d(x,\Sigma_{0})\geqslant r.\end{array}\right.

Notice that the density is smoothed in a different way in order to get the compatibility conditions.
Now, by assumption, we have
\chi_{r}\rho_{0}\in C^{1+m}
, where
m\in(0,1)
. Also, it is clear that
v_{0,n}
and
(1-\chi_{r})S_{n}\rho_{0}
are smooth and belong to
C^{1+m}
. Then, we can apply the result of
[
16
]
and construct, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
, a unique maximal solution
v_{n},\rho_{n}\in C\big([0,T^{\star}_{n});C^{1+m}\big).

➋
Boundedness of the initial data.

The first thing to check is that the initial data of the scheme (
3.38
) satisfy the assumptions in Theorem
3.1
and Proposition
3.1
, uniformly with respect to
n
. Let us first check that

\|\omega_{0,n}\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}},\|\rho_{0,n}\|_{W^{1,a}\cap W^{1,\infty}}

and

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1]}}h^{\beta}\Big(\|\omega_{0,n}\|^{s}_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},\mathcal{X}_{0,h}}+\|\rho_{0,n}\|^{s+1}_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},\mathcal{X}_{0,h}}\Big)

(3.39)

are uniformly bounded with respect to
n
.
The first claim follows from the uniform continuity of the operator
S_{n}:L^{p}\to L^{p}
. As to the boundedness of (
3.39
), we proceed as follows. We repeat first the arguments in pages 62-63 from
[
17
]
allowing to get

\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\omega_{0,n}\|_{s-1}\leqslant C\big(\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\omega_{0}\|_{s-1}+\widetilde{\|}X_{0,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big).

(3.40)

On the other hand, for the second term in (
3.40
), we write first

\displaystyle\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\rho_{0,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\big(\chi_{r}\rho_{0}\big)-S_{n}\rho_{0}\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\big(\chi_{r}\big)+(1-\chi_{r})\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\big(S_{n}\rho_{0}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\big(\chi_{r}\rho_{0}\big)-S_{n}\rho_{0}\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\big(\chi_{r}\big)+(1-\chi_{r})\Bigg(S_{n}\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\rho_{0}+[\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}},S_{n}]\rho_{0}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{J}_{i}.

The estimates of the first three terms are straightforward and one gets

\sum_{i=1}^{3}\|\mathcal{J}_{i}\|_{s}\leqslant C\big(\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}+\|X_{0,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\|\rho_{0}\|_{s}\big),

where,
C
depend only on the
C^{s+1}
-norm of
\chi_{r}
.
As to the estimate
\mathcal{J}_{4}
, we use
[
39
, Proposition 8]
in order to find

\|\mathcal{J}_{4}\|_{s}\leqslant C\big(\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}+\widetilde{\|}X_{0,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big),

Consequently, we end up with

\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\rho_{0,n}\|_{s}\leqslant C\big(\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\rho_{0}\|_{s}+\widetilde{\|}X_{0,\lambda,h}\|_{s}\|\rho_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\big).

(3.41)

Finally, (
3.39
) follows from (
3.40
), (
3.41
) and the assumptions in Theorem
3.1
on
\rho_{0}
and
X_{0,\lambda,h}.

Next, the assumptions
{(\nabla\rho_{0})}_{|_{\Sigma_{0}}}=0
and
\chi_{r}\rho_{0}\in C^{1+m}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
ensure the platitude condition (
3.6
) for the regularized initial density
\widetilde{S}_{n}\rho_{0}
. Indeed, we have by construction

\widetilde{S}_{n}\rho_{0}(x)=\rho_{0}(x),\quad\forall x\in(\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{r}{2},}

implying that we can replace
\widetilde{S}_{n}\rho_{0}
by
\rho_{0}
in the neighborhood
(\Sigma_{0})_{\frac{r}{2}}.

Since
(\nabla\rho_{0})_{|_{\Sigma_{0}}}=0
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{\underset{x\neq y,\;|x-y|<\frac{r}{2}}{y\in\Sigma_{0}}}\frac{\left|\nabla\rho_{0}(x)\right|}{|x-y|^{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\underset{x\neq y,\;|x-y|<\frac{r}{2}}{y\in\Sigma_{0}}}\frac{\left|\nabla\rho_{0}(x)-\nabla\rho_{0}(y)\right|}{|x-y|^{m}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{C^{m}((\Sigma_{0})_{r})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{1+m}((\Sigma_{0})_{r})}.

➌
Uniform lower bound on the lifespan
T_{n}^{\star}
.
This can be done by virtue of Corollary
3.1
and Proposition
3.3
leading to

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star},\quad T<T_{n}^{\star},

where
T
is the unique solution to
\Gamma(T)=m
and
\Gamma
is defined in (
3.24
). Notice that the constant
C_{0}
in (
3.24
) is independent of
n
.
Consequently, we find that

{\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}T_{n}^{\star}}\geqslant T>0\quad\quad\text{and }\quad\quad v_{n},\rho_{n}\in C\big([0,T],C^{1+m}\big),\;\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}.

(3.42)

Once we get (
3.42
), we can justify again all the a priori estimates proved in the previous propositions since the approximate solution
(v_{n},\rho_{n})
is smooth enough on
[0,T].
The next step consists then in proving uniform estimates, with respect to
n
, on the time interval
[0,T].

➍
Uniform boundedness of the approximated solutions.

Combining the results of ➋, Propositions
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
we obtain the following uniform bounds in
n
, for all
t\in[0,T]

\|\omega_{n}(t)\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}}+\|\rho_{n}(t)\|_{W^{1,a}\cap W^{1,\infty}}\leqslant C_{0,T}

\displaystyle\|v_{n}(t)\|_{LL}+\|v_{n}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t,n})}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{0,T},

(3.43)

and

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta_{n}(t)}\Big(\|\rho_{n}(t)\|^{s+1}_{\Sigma_{t,n},\mathcal{X}_{t,h,n}}+\|\omega_{n}(t)\|^{s}_{\Sigma_{t,n},\mathcal{X}_{t,h,n}}+\widetilde{\|}\mathcal{X}_{t,h,n}\|_{s}\Big)\leqslant C_{0,T},

(3.44)

where,
\beta_{n}(t)
is given in accordance with (
3.28
) as

\beta_{n}(t)\triangleq\beta+C\int_{0}^{t}W_{n}(\tau)d\tau

(3.45)

and

W_{n}(t)\triangleq\Big(\|v_{n}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t,n})}+\|\omega_{n}(t)\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}}\Big)\exp\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\|v_{n}(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau\bigg).

(3.46)

Above, we use the notation

\Sigma_{t,n}=\Psi_{n}(t,\Sigma_{0}),

with

\Psi_{n}(t,x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}v_{n}\big(\tau,\Psi_{n}(\tau,x)\big)d\tau,

and
\mathcal{X}_{t,h,n}\triangleq(X_{t,\lambda,h,n})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
which is given by

X_{t,\lambda,h,n}=\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{n}(t,\Psi_{n}^{-1}(t,\cdot)).

Using (
3.43
) together with classical regularity of the flow associated to Yudovich solutions, we get uniformly with respect to
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\Psi_{n}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];\hbox{Id}+C^{\exp(-C_{0,T})}),

while, (
2.19
) implies

I\big((\Sigma_{t,n})_{\delta_{t,n}^{-1}(h)},(\mathcal{X}_{t,n})_{h}\big)\geqslant I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}\big)h^{\int_{0}^{t}W_{n}(\tau)d\tau},\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(3.47)

where, we set

\delta_{t,n}^{-1}(h)\triangleq h^{\exp(-\int_{0}^{t}\|v_{n}(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau)}.

(3.48)

Remark that, due to (
3.30
), (
3.43
) and the uniform bounds on the initial data proved in ➋, we have

\int_{0}^{t}W_{n}(\tau)d\tau\leqslant\Gamma(T)=m,\quad\forall t\in[0,T]

and

\delta_{t,n}^{-1}(h)\leqslant h^{\exp(-C_{0,T})}\triangleq h^{\alpha_{1}}.

Hence, (
3.44
), (
3.45
) and (
3.47
) imply

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta+Cm}\Big(\|\rho_{n}(t)\|^{s+1}_{\Sigma_{t,n},\mathcal{X}_{t,h,n}}+\|\omega_{n}(t)\|^{s}_{\Sigma_{t,n},\mathcal{X}_{t,h,n}}+\widetilde{\|}\mathcal{X}_{t,h,n}\|_{s}\Big)\leqslant C_{0,T},

(3.49)

h^{-(\gamma+Cm)}I\big((\Sigma_{t,n})_{h^{\alpha_{1}}},(\mathcal{X}_{t,n})_{h}\big)\geqslant\inf_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{-\gamma}I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})_{h}\big)>0,\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

(3.50)

In order to proceed, we have to understand the compactness of the sequences
(\Psi_{n}(t,\cdot),\mathcal{X}_{t,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}

➎
Stability estimates and convergence.

A direct application of Corollary
5.1
shows that
(v_{n},\rho_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{2})
. More precisely, we have

\|v_{n}(t)-v_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho_{n}(t)-\rho_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant{C}_{0,T}(\|v_{0,n}-v_{0,m}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho_{0,n}-\rho_{0,m}\|_{L^{2}})^{\exp(-{C}_{0,T})},\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

where,
{C}_{0,T}
is a constant depending only on the size of the initial data and
T
and it comes in particular from the a priori estimates of
(v_{n},\rho_{n})
. This is enough to conclude that
(v_{n},\rho_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}).

Mixing this strong convergence result with the uniform bounds obtained in ➍ allow to pass to the limit and construct a local solution
(\omega,\rho)
to the problem (
3.1
). In addition, this solution satisfies

(\omega,\rho)\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{a}\cap L^{\infty})\times L^{\infty}([0,T];W^{1,a}\cap W^{1\infty}).

On the other hand, since
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
, together with the bounds of the
L^{p}
-norms of
\omega_{n}
, yields the strong convergence of
v_{n}
towards
v
in
L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty})
. This allows to get the following strong convergence of the flow sequence
(\Psi_{n})
, for more details see for instance
[
17
, Lemma 5.5.2]

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\Psi_{n}}=\Psi\quad\text{in }\quad L^{\infty}\Big([0,T];\hbox{Id}+C^{\exp\left(-\varepsilon-\int_{0}^{T}\|v(\tau,\cdot)\|_{LL}d\tau\right)}\Big).

In fact, we can prove that the convergence holds in the following sense

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\Psi_{n}}=\Psi\quad\text{in }\quad C\Big([0,T];\hbox{Id}+C(\mathbb{R}^{2})\Big).

(3.51)

Consequently, we find that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]\atop x\in\Sigma_{0}}|\Psi_{n}(t,x)-\Psi(t,x)|=0.

Therefore, for any
h>0
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for all
n\geqslant n_{0}
and for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(\Sigma_{t})_{2h}^{c}\subset(\Sigma_{t,n})_{h}^{c}.

Together with (
3.43
), we infer that for all
t\in[0,T]
and
h\in(0,e^{-1}]

\|\nabla v_{n}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{2h}^{c})}\leqslant\|\nabla v_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t,n})_{h}^{c})}\leqslant(-\log h)C_{0,T}.

Hence, for any
x,y\in(\Sigma_{t})_{3h}^{c}
, with
|x-y|\leqslant h
we obtain that the segment
[x,y]\subset(\Sigma_{t})_{2h}^{c}
. This implies by virtue of the mean value theorem

|v_{n}(x)-v_{n}(y)|\leqslant(-\log h)C_{0,T}|x-y|.

Let
n
go to infinity and
y
to
x
, we end up with

\frac{\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t})_{3h}^{c})}}{-\log h}\leqslant C_{0,T}.

This leads to

\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}{\leqslant}\,C_{0,T}.

On the other hand, it is easy to check that

\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}\leqslant\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|v_{n}(\tau)\|_{LL}

and

\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}}\leqslant\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\omega_{n}(t)\|_{L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}}.

The next step consists to prove that, for all
(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}]

\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{s})

and to check that for all
s^{\prime}<s
,
h\in(0,e^{-1}]
and uniformly with respect to
\lambda\in\Lambda
, there holds

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{n}=\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi\quad\text{in }\quad L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{s^{\prime}}).

(3.52)

This can be done by using an interpolation inequality, the limit (
3.51
), and the fact that the sequence
\left(\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{s})
, for all
(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}]
. More precisely, one gets

\displaystyle\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{n}-\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}C^{s^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{n}-\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}{B_{\infty,\infty}^{-1}}}^{\delta}\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{n}-\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}C^{s}}^{1-\delta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|({X_{0,\lambda,h}},\textnormal{div}{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\big)\big\|^{\delta}_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}\|\Psi_{n}-\Psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}{L^{\infty}}}^{\delta}\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}C^{s}}^{1-\delta},

for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, which is enough to obtain the desired result
3.52
.

Now, Corollary
3.2
insures that the vector fields

X_{t,h,\lambda}=(\partial_{X_{0,h,\lambda}}\Psi)(t,\Psi(t,\cdot))

belong to
C^{s}
, for all
t\in[0,T]
. Furthermore, they are admissible in the sense of Definition
2.5
, for all
t\in[0,T]
,. This can be proved by virtue of the strong convergence of the flow (
3.51
) combined with (
3.50
) .
Finally, straightforward arguments based on (
3.49
) together with the aforementioned convergence results for
\rho_{n},\omega_{n}
and
X_{t,h,\lambda,n}
yield

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]\atop t\in[0,T]}h^{\beta+Cm}\Big(\|\rho(t)\|^{s+1}_{\Sigma_{t},\mathcal{X}_{t,h}}+\|\omega(t)\|^{s}_{\Sigma_{t},\mathcal{X}_{t,h}}+\widetilde{\|}\mathcal{X}_{t,h}\|_{s}\Big)\leqslant C_{0,T}.

This achieves the estimates for the constructed solution.

➏
Uniqueness of the solutions.

The proof can be done along the same lines as in
[
39
]
. More precisely, it suffices to use the stability estimates mentioned in ➎.
∎

4.
Viscous case

In this section, we shall examine the partial viscous Boussinesq system written in terms of vorticity and density formulation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\omega_{\kappa}+v\cdot\nabla\omega_{\kappa}=\partial_{1}\rho_{\kappa}\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},&\\
\partial_{t}\rho_{\kappa}+v_{\kappa}\cdot\nabla\rho_{\kappa}-\kappa\Delta\rho_{\kappa}=0,&\\
v_{\kappa}=\nabla^{\perp}\Delta^{-1}\omega_{\kappa},&\\
(\omega_{\kappa},\rho_{\kappa})|_{t=0}=(\omega_{0},\rho_{0}).\end{array}\right.

(4.1)

We aim to establish a local well-posedness result for a large class of initial data allowing in particular singular vortex patches as in the previous section. However, we want to get estimates uniformly with respect to the vanishing diffusion parameter
\kappa\in(0,1)
, which will induce several new difficulties compared to the inviscid case.

4.1.
General statement and consequence

We intend to discuss a general statement covering more cases than the soft version detailed in Theorem
1.2
and restricted to the singular vortex patches. The main result of this section reads as follows.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(s,\varepsilon,p)\in(0,1)\times(0,\frac{s}{2})\times(\frac{1}{\frac{s}{2}-\varepsilon},\infty)
and
(a,m)\in[2,\infty)\times(1,2).
Consider a compact negligible set of the plane
\Sigma_{0}
satisfying the integrability condition (
4.9
). Let
v_{0}
be a divergence-free vector field with vorticity
\omega_{0}\in L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}
and
\rho_{0}
be a real-valued function in
W^{2,a}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap C^{1+m}((\Sigma_{0})_{r})
, for some
r>0
such that
\nabla\rho_{0}
and
{\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}}
are vanishing on
\Sigma_{0}.
Consider a family of vector fields
\mathcal{X}_{0}=(X_{0,\lambda,h})_{(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}]}
of class
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
as well as their divergences and suppose that this family is
\Sigma_{0}
-admissible of order
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
with

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\Big(\|\omega_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})}^{s,p}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\mathcal{X}_{0})}^{s+1,p}\Big)<\infty.

Then, there exists
T>0
independent of
\kappa\in(0,1)
such that the viscous Boussinesq system (
4.1
) has a unique solution

(\omega_{\kappa},\rho_{\kappa})\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L^{a}\cap L^{\infty}\big)\times L^{\infty}\big([0,T];W^{1,a}\cap W^{1,\infty}\big)

with uniform bounds on
\kappa\in(0,1)
and
satisfying in addition

\sup_{\underset{\kappa\in(0,1)}{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}}\frac{\|\nabla v_{\kappa}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}((\Sigma_{t,\kappa})_{h}^{c})}}{-\log h}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]).

Furthermore,
there exists
C_{0,T}>0
, depending only on the initial data,
\Sigma_{0}
and
T
, and there exists a triplet of constants
\Xi=(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})
, such that the following results hold.
By denoting

\sigma_{t}=s\big(1-\tfrac{\varepsilon t}{sT}\big)-\varepsilon,

we get for any
t\in[0,T]

\sup_{\underset{\kappa\in(0,1)}{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}}h^{\beta_{1}}\|\omega_{\kappa}(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t})_{{h^{\alpha_{1}}}},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h,\kappa})}^{\sigma_{t},p}+\sup_{\underset{\kappa\in(0,1)}{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}}h^{\beta_{1}}\|\rho_{\kappa}(t)\|_{(\Sigma_{t,\kappa})_{{h^{\alpha_{1}}}},(\mathcal{X}_{t,h,\kappa})}^{\sigma_{t,\kappa}+1,p}\leqslant C_{0,T}.

Here,
\Sigma_{t,\kappa}
is given by

\Sigma_{t,\kappa}\triangleq\Psi_{\kappa}(t,\Sigma_{0})

and the transported vector field
\mathcal{X}_{t,\kappa}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
by the flow
\Psi_{\kappa}(t)
, defined by

X_{t,\lambda,h,\kappa}\triangleq\big(\partial_{X_{0,\lambda,h}}\Psi_{\kappa}\big)(t,\Psi^{-1}_{\kappa}(t,\cdot)),

is
\Sigma_{t}
–admissible of order
\Xi
and belongs to
B^{\sigma_{t}}_{p,\infty}
, for all
t\in[0,T],(\lambda,h)\in\Lambda\times(0,e^{-1}]
, uniformly with respect to
\kappa
. In addition,
X_{t,\lambda,h,\kappa}(\Psi_{\kappa}(t,\cdot))
belongs to
C^{\sigma_{t}-\frac{2}{p}}
.

Remark 4.1
.

For the sake of simplicity, we shall drop the index
\kappa
from the system (
4.1
).

Before giving the proof of this general result, we shall sketch the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

The proof in the viscous case differs slightly from the inviscid one, because we have to deal with Besov spaces. We shall follow the same lines as in the inviscid case. First, we assume that the boundary of the initial patch is smooth except at some closed singular set, where the singularity is algebraic. More precisely, we assume that

\partial\Omega_{0}=\big\{x\in V:f_{0}(x)=0\big\},

for a small neighborhood
V
of
\partial\Omega_{0}
, and for a function
f_{0}
in
C^{1+s}
satisfying, for some
\widetilde{\gamma}>0

|\nabla f_{0}(x)|\geqslant d(x,\Sigma_{0})^{{\gamma}},\quad\forall x\in V.

(4.2)

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
f_{0}
is compactly supported. Let
(\theta_{h})_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}
be a family of functions of class
C^{\infty}_{c}
, with
\theta_{h}\equiv 1
in
(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}
and supported in
(\Sigma_{0})_{h^{\alpha}}^{c}
, for some
\alpha>1
, which satisfies for all positive real number
\tilde{s}

\|\theta_{h}\|_{C^{\tilde{s}}}\leqslant C_{\tilde{s}}h^{-\tilde{s}}.

Let
V_{1}
be an open set containing
\partial\Omega_{0}
and its closure set
\overline{V}_{1}
is contained in
V
, that is
\overline{V}_{1}\subset V
. Consider a
C^{\infty}
function
\widetilde{\theta}
with
\widetilde{\theta}\equiv 1
in
V_{1}
and
\textnormal{supp}\,\widetilde{\theta}\subset V
. Define the family
\mathcal{X}_{0,h}\triangleq(X_{0,0,h},X_{0,1,h})
defined through

X_{0,0,h}=\nabla^{\perp}(\theta_{h}f_{0}),\quad X_{0,1,h}=\theta_{h}(1-\widetilde{\theta})\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

From this construction together with the assumption (
4.2
) we obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{-\gamma}I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\mathcal{X}_{0,h}\big)>0.

(4.3)

Then, due to Lemma
2.4
and the fact that
f_{0}
is compactly supported, we deduce that for
h\in(0,e^{-1}]

\widetilde{\|}X_{0,0,h}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\leqslant C\widetilde{\|}X_{0,0,h}\|_{C^{s}},

(4.4)

where the constant
C
depends only on the support of
f_{0}
. This asserts that
X_{0,0,h},\textnormal{div }X_{0,0,h}\in B^{s}_{p,\infty}
. Unfortunately, the vector field
X_{0,1,h}
belongs to
C^{s}
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, but not to
B^{s}_{p,\infty}
. To remedy this defect, we shall make use of the partition of the unity given by Lemma
2.5
. From the vector field
X_{0,1,h}
, we construct a countable family
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h}\triangleq(\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}
to be defined as

\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h}(x)=\psi_{n}(x)X_{0,1,h}(x),\quad\forall(n,x)\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},

where the functions
\psi_{n}
are introduced in Lemma
2.5
. In particular, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
,
\psi_{n}
is compactly supported in
\mathcal{O}_{n}
, with
|\mathcal{O}_{n}|\lesssim 1
. By the product laws stated in Lemma
2.3
, we have

\widetilde{\|}\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\big(\|\psi_{n}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}+\|\nabla\psi_{n}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\big)\widetilde{\|}X_{0,1,h}\|_{C^{s}},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}.

On the other hand, by Sobolev embeddings and (
2.7
), we may write

\|\psi_{n}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}+\|\nabla\psi_{n}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|\psi_{n}\|_{L^{p}}+\|\nabla\psi_{n}\|_{L^{p}}+\|\nabla^{2}\psi_{n}\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim 1,\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}.

Thus, we end up with

\widetilde{\|}\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\widetilde{\|}X_{0,1,h}\|_{C^{s}},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}.

(4.5)

Now, by denoting
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,0,h}\triangleq X_{0,0,h}
, then, our next task is to show that the family
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h}=(\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\Sigma_{0}
- admissible of order
\Xi
in the sense of Definition
2.5
, for some
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
. Indeed, an elementary computation yields

\displaystyle I((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h}(x)|

(4.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in(\Sigma_{0})_{h}^{c}}\max\big\{|X_{0,0,h}(x)|\;;\;\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}\psi_{n}(x)|X_{0,1,h}(x)|\big\}.

Meanwhile, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, Lemma
2.5
gives

\displaystyle 1=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}\psi_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}:x\in\mathscr{O}_{n}\}}\psi_{n}(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle N_{d}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}\psi_{n}(x).

Consequently,

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}}\psi_{n}(x)\geq\frac{1}{N_{d}},\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Plugging this inequality in (
4.6
) yields

\displaystyle I((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})\geqslant\frac{1}{N_{d}}I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\mathcal{X}_{0,h}\big).

Combining this estimate with (
4.2
) allows to get

\inf_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{-\gamma}I((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})>0.

Finally, to achieve the proof of the admissibility of the family
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h}
, as stated in the Definition
2.5
, we need to show the existence of a positive number
\beta>0
such that

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}N_{s,p}((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})<\infty.

(4.7)

Indeed, by the definition of
N_{s,p}
and (
4.5
), we have

\displaystyle N_{s,p}((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\widetilde{\|}\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}}{I((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\max_{j\in\{0,1\}}\frac{\widetilde{\|}X_{0,j,h}\|_{C^{s}}}{I\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h}\big)}

\displaystyle\leqslant CN_{s}\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\mathcal{X}_{0,h}\big).

Similar arguments as in
[
17
,
39
]
show that

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}N_{s}\big((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\mathcal{X}_{0,h}\big)<\infty,

(4.8)

for some index
\beta>0
, leading to the claim (
4.7
). All in all, we conclude that the family
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h}
is
\Sigma_{0}
–admissible of order
\Xi=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
. It remains to check that

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\Big(\|\omega_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})}^{s,p}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})}^{s+1,p}\Big)<\infty.

A direct computation shows that

\partial_{\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h}}\omega_{0}=0,\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N},

which obviously gives in view of (
4.8
)

\sup_{h\in(0,e^{-1}]}h^{\beta}\|\omega_{0}\|_{(\Sigma_{0})_{h},(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})}^{s,p}<\infty.

As to the estimate of the density, we apply Lemma
2.4
together with (
4.4
), (
4.5
) and (
4.8
)

\displaystyle h^{\beta}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\|\partial_{\widetilde{X}_{0,n,h}}\rho_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}}{I((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})}\leqslant h^{\beta}N_{s}((\Sigma_{0})_{h},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0,h})\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{s+1}}.

This ends the proof of the desired estimate.
Therefore, the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
are completely fulfilled and as a consequence we get the local well-posedness in Theorem
1.2
. Besides, Theorem
4.1
asserts that, for all
h\in(0,e^{-1}]
and for all
t\in[0,T]
,

X_{t,0,h}(\Psi(t,\cdot))\in C^{\sigma_{t}-\frac{2}{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow C^{s-2\varepsilon-\frac{2}{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

By taking
\varepsilon
close to
0
and
p
large enough we obtain
X_{t,0,h}(\Psi(t,\cdot))\in C^{s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
s^{\prime}<s
sufficiently close to
s
.
Then the proof of the regularity persistence of the boundary
\Psi(t,\partial\Omega_{0}\backslash\Sigma_{0})
follows from the same arguments detailed in the inviscid case, see Section
3
. This ends the proof of Theorem
1.2
provided that Theorem
4.1
is true.
∎

4.2.
A priori estimates

As explained in the introduction, the viscous case is more delicate compared to the inviscid one and requires intricate analysis. The major drawback arises from the fact that the local information, such as the platitude assumption (
3.6
), will be diffused by the dissipation mechanism. To elucidate the competition between the advection and the dissipation we need suitable ansatz showing up different action regimes.

4.2.1.
Reduction to transport-diffusion equation with a singular potential

We shall examine weak
L^{p}
-estimates for
\nabla\rho
and
\omega
with uniform estimates on the conductivity when the initial vorticity is singular. This part is delicate and requires an appropriate ansatz that we should handle through suitable estimates on transport-diffusion equation with logarithmic singular potential. The main statement reads as follows.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\Sigma_{0}
be a compact negligible set in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying, for some
\sigma>0
and for all small
\epsilon\in(0,1),

\int_{d(x,\Sigma_{0})<\epsilon}\log^{4}\big(d(x,\Sigma_{0})\big)dx\lesssim\epsilon^{\sigma}.

(4.9)

Let
\omega_{0}\in L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}
,
m\in(1,2)
and
\rho_{0}
be a scalar function satisfying

\nabla\rho_{0},\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\in L^{2}\cap L^{\infty},

supplemented with the platitude conditions

\sup_{x\notin\Sigma_{0}}(\varphi_{0}(x))^{-m}\left|\nabla\rho_{0}(x)\right|<\infty,\quad\quad\sup_{x\notin\Sigma_{0}}(\varphi_{0}(x))^{-m+1}\left|\nabla|\nabla\rho_{0}(x)|^{2}\right|<\infty,

(4.10)

where
\varphi_{0}
is given by (
2.4
). Consider a smooth maximal solution
(\omega,\rho)
to (
4.1
) defined on
[0,T^{\star})
and introduce the function
t\in[0,T^{\star})\mapsto\mathbb{V}(t)

\mathbb{V}(t)\triangleq\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL\cap L(\Sigma_{\tau})}d\tau.

(4.11)

There exists
C_{0}>0
depending only on the initial data and
\Sigma_{0}
and there exists
C_{\sigma,m}>0
depending only on
\sigma
and
m
such that the following holds.

If
{\mathbb{V}(T)}\leqslant C_{\sigma,m}
, for some
T\in(0,T^{\star})
, then for all
p\in[2,\infty]
, we have

\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad\|\nabla\rho(t)\|_{L^{p}}+\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant G(t),

(4.12)

with

G(t)\triangleq C_{0}(1+t)^{3}\left(1+\|A\|_{L^{6}_{t}}\exp\left(C\|A\|_{L^{2}_{t}}^{2}\right)\right)

(4.13)

and

A(t)\triangleq\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}.

(4.14)

In addition, if
v_{0}\triangleq\nabla^{\perp}\Delta^{-1}\omega_{0}\in L^{\infty}
then

\forall\,t\in[0,T],\quad\|v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant CG(t)e^{tG(t)}.

(4.15)

Moreover, we have the blowup criterion,

T^{\star}<\infty\Longrightarrow\lim_{t\to T^{\star}}{\mathbb{V}(t)}>C_{\sigma,m}.

(4.16)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\rho_{0}
is not a constant function. Otherwise the system (
4.1
) reduces to Euler equations where the global regularity persistence is proved in
[
17
]
. In the first step, we shall proceed as in Proposition
3.1
. Actually, an elementary calculus allows us to check that

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)|\nabla\rho|^{2}+2{\kappa}\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\nabla\partial_{i}\rho|^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\partial_{i}v)\cdot\nabla\rho\,\partial_{i}\rho

\displaystyle\leqslant 2|\nabla v||\nabla\rho|^{2}.

Hence, in view of (
2.2
), the function
\bar{\varrho}\triangleq|\nabla\rho|^{2}
satisfies the inequality

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\bar{\varrho}(t,x)\leqslant 2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\bar{\varrho}(t,x).

(4.17)

Now, we introduce the splitting

\bar{\varrho}(t,x)=\phi(t,x)\,\eta(t,x),

with
\phi
and
\eta
are solution and sub-solution of the following problems

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\phi(t,x)=2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\phi(t,x),&\\
\phi|_{t=0}=|\nabla\rho_{0}|^{2}\end{array}\right.

(4.18)

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+\big(v-2\kappa\nabla\log(\phi)\big)\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\eta(t,x)\leqslant 0,&\\
\eta|_{t=0}=1.\end{array}\right.

(4.19)

Note that by construction
\bar{\varrho},\phi\geqslant 0
, implying in particular that
\eta\geqslant 0,
and the decomposition of
\bar{\varrho}
is justified by the fact that

\phi(t,\cdot)>0,\quad\forall t>0.

(4.20)

The proof of (
4.20
) can be checked using the fundamental solution for the convection-diffusion equation. In fact, according to
[
38
, Section 3]
, see, also
[
59
]
, any smooth solution
\phi
to (
4.18
) can be represented through

\phi(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma_{\kappa}(t,x;0,y)\phi_{0}(y)dy+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma_{\kappa}(t,x;s,y)f(s,y)\phi(s,y)dyds,

(4.21)

where

f(t,x)=2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\geqslant 0,\quad\phi_{0}=|\nabla\rho_{0}|^{2}

and
\Gamma_{\kappa}(t,x;t^{\prime},x^{\prime})
is the unique solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}-\kappa\Delta_{x}\right)\Gamma_{\kappa}=0,&(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{4},\quad 0\leqslant t^{\prime}<t,\\
\Gamma_{\kappa}(t^{\prime},x;t^{\prime},x^{\prime})=\delta(x-x^{\prime}).\end{array}\right.

We also note that, for any
0\leqslant t^{\prime}<t,
the function
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto\Gamma_{\kappa}(t,x;t^{\prime},x^{\prime})
is continuous and satisfies

\Gamma_{\kappa}(t,x;t^{\prime},x^{\prime})>0.

(4.22)

We recall that both functions
\phi_{0}
and
f\phi
are positive. Hence, if
\phi(t_{0},x_{0})
vanishes for some point
(t_{0},x_{0})\in(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then thanks to the integral representation and (
4.21
), this would imply that

\phi_{0}(y)=0,\quad\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

This contradicts the fact that the initial density is non-constant. All in all, we deduce our claim (
4.20
). Our next claim is to show that

\|\eta(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1,\quad\forall t\in[0,T^{\star}).

(4.23)

To do so, we rewrite (
4.19
) as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+(v-V_{\kappa})\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\eta=-F,&\\
\eta|_{t=0}=1,\end{array}\right.

(4.24)

where
F
is a positive smooth function and
V_{\kappa}\triangleq 2\kappa\nabla\log(\varphi)
. Let
\Phi
be a smooth and non–negative function satisfying the following

•

\Phi
is integrable, that is,
\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi(x)dx<\infty}.

•

\Phi(x)\geqslant 1,
for all
|x|\leqslant 1.

•

There exists a constant
C>0
, such that

\frac{\left|\nabla\Phi(x)\right|}{\Phi(x)}\leqslant C,\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(4.25)

Define for
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star},

\Phi_{n}(x)\triangleq\Phi\left(\frac{x}{n}\right).

Then, multiplying (
4.24
) by
\eta^{p-1}\Phi_{n}
, for
p\geqslant 2
and integrating by parts over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we deduce in view of the divergence-free condition of
v

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\eta^{p}\Phi_{n}dx+(p-1)\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\eta^{p-2}\Phi_{n}dx

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\textnormal{div }V_{k}|\eta^{p}\Phi_{n}dx+\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|v-V_{k}|\eta^{p}\left|\nabla\Phi_{n}\right|dx

(4.26)

\displaystyle+\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\eta|\eta^{p-1}\left|\nabla\Phi_{n}\right|dx.

Hence, Cauchy-Schwarz inequality combined with (
4.25
) and Young inequality provide

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\eta|\eta^{p-1}\left|\nabla\Phi_{n}\right|dx

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{C}{n}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\eta^{p-2}\Phi_{n}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\eta^{p}\Phi_{n}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\eta^{p-2}\Phi_{n}dx+\frac{C^{2}}{n^{2}(p-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\eta^{p}\Phi_{n}dx.

Plugging this inequality into (
4.26
) and exploiting again (
4.25
), we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\eta^{p}\Phi_{n}dx\leqslant\Xi_{n}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\eta^{p}\Phi_{n}dx,

with,

\Xi_{n}(t)\triangleq\|\textnormal{div }V_{k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\frac{C}{n}\|v(t)-V_{k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\frac{2\kappa C^{2}}{n^{2}}\cdot

Therefore, Gronwall lemma together with the estimate
\Phi_{n}(x)\geqslant 1
for all
|x|\leqslant n,
imply

\left(\int_{|x|\leqslant n}\eta^{p}(t,x)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\eta^{p}(t,x)\Phi_{n}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi_{n}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{p}\int_{0}^{t}\Xi_{n}(\tau)d\tau\right).

Consequently, by letting
p
go to infinity allows to get for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}

0\leqslant\sup_{|x|\leqslant n}\eta(t,x)\leqslant 1.

Finally, taking
n
to
\infty
yields (
4.23
).
Let us now estimate in
L^{p}
the solution
\phi
to (
4.18
). For this aim, we introduce the decomposition

\phi=\bar{\varphi}+\psi,

with

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)\bar{\varphi}(t,x)=2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\bar{\varphi}(t,x),&\\
\bar{\varphi}|_{t=0}=|\nabla\rho_{0}|^{2}\end{array}\right.

(4.27)

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta\big)\psi(t,x)=2\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\psi(t,x)+\kappa\Delta\bar{\varphi},&\\
\psi|_{t=0}=0.\end{array}\right.

(4.28)

Remark that, according to (
4.27
), the quantity
\bar{\varphi}
satisfies in particular (
3.10
) treated in details in the inviscid case and the estimates (
3.16
) and (
3.18
) can be applied. In particular, if
t\in[0,T^{\star})\mapsto m(t)
is a function satisfying

\forall t\in[0,T^{\star}),\quad m^{\prime}(t)+\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}\leqslant 0.

(4.29)

then we get for any
p\in[2,\infty]
, for any
t\in\mathtt{I},

\quad\|\bar{\varphi}(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant C_{p},

\|\big(\varphi_{t}(\cdot)\big)^{-m(t)}\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leqslant C_{p},

(4.30)

where
\varphi_{t}
is given by (
3.8
) and

\mathtt{I}\triangleq\big\{t\in[0,T^{\star}),m(t)\geqslant 0\big\},\quad C_{p}\triangleq\|\varphi_{0}^{-m}(\cdot)\nabla\rho_{0}(\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}

We point out that
m
is a continuous non-increasing function, which implies that the set
\mathtt{I}
is an interval.
On the other hand, the inequality (
4.29
) occurs if we impose the strong condition

\forall\,t\in[0,T^{\star}),\quad m^{\prime}(t)+\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(t)e^{\mathbb{V}(t)}\leqslant 0\quad\hbox{with}\quad\mathbb{V}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\|{v}(\tau)\|_{LL\cap L(\Sigma_{\tau})}d\tau.

(4.31)

Let us now focus on the estimate of the
L^{p}
-norm of
\nabla\bar{\varphi}.
Employing the following fact,

|\nabla v(t,x)|\leqslant\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t})),

which is a consequence of (
2.2
) and differentiating in
x

the equation (
4.27
) we infer after some elementary computations

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)|\nabla\bar{\varphi}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 6\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,|\nabla\bar{\varphi}|^{2}+4M(t,x)\left|\nabla\bar{\varphi}\right|\frac{\bar{\varphi}}{d(x,\Sigma_{t})},

(4.32)

where, we set

M(t,x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|\nabla d(x,\Sigma_{t})|&\text{ if }d(x,\Sigma_{t})<e^{-1},\\
0&\text{ if }d(x,\Sigma_{t})\geqslant e^{-1}.\end{array}\right.

In order to estimate the term
\frac{1}{d(x,\Sigma_{t})},
we apply Lemma
2.6
with fixed
h
given by

h=\min\big\{d(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}),e^{-1}\big\}=\varphi_{t}(x)

getting

d(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0})\geqslant h\Longrightarrow d(x,\Sigma_{t})\geqslant h^{e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}}=\left(\varphi_{t}(x)\right)^{e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}}.

Thus, we get

\frac{1}{d(x,\Sigma_{t})}\leqslant\left(\varphi_{t}(x)\right)^{-e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}},\quad\forall x\notin\Sigma_{t}

and (
4.32
) guides to

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)|\nabla\bar{\varphi}|^{2}\leqslant

\displaystyle 6\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,|\nabla\bar{\varphi}|^{2}

\displaystyle+4M(t,x)\left|\nabla\bar{\varphi}\right|\bar{\varphi}\left(\varphi_{t}(x)\right)^{-e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}}.

(4.33)

Let
t\in[0,T^{\star})\mapsto\gamma(t)
be a
C^{1}
function, to be chosen later, and introduce

\widetilde{\varrho}(t,x)\triangleq\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{-\gamma(t)}|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,x)|^{2}.

(4.34)

One can scrutinize from (
3.8
) that

\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\big)\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{-\gamma(t)}=-\gamma^{\prime}(t)\ln\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{-\gamma(t)}.

Therefore, in sight of (
4.2.1
), it holds

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\widetilde{\varrho}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\Big(\gamma^{\prime}(t)\ln^{+}\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)+6\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\Big)\widetilde{\varrho}

(4.35)

\displaystyle+4M(t,x)\sqrt{\widetilde{\varrho}}\;\bar{\varphi}\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{{-\frac{\gamma(t)}{2}}-e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}}.

To handle the first part on the r.h.s. above we follow the same lines as in the inviscid case. Actually, by using the fact
\ln^{+}(\varphi_{t}(x))=\ln^{+}\big(d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)\big)
combined with Lemma
2.7
we find

\displaystyle\ln^{+}\big(d\big(x,\Sigma_{t}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\ln^{+}\big(d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)\big)\,e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}.

(4.36)

Inserting this estimate into (
4.35
) gives

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\widetilde{\varrho}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\ln^{+}\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)\Big(\gamma^{\prime}(t)+6\|{v}(t)\|_{L(\Sigma_{t})}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}\Big)\widetilde{\varrho}

\displaystyle+4M(t,x)\sqrt{\widetilde{\varrho}}\;\bar{\varphi}\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{{-\gamma(t)\over 2}-e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|v(\tau)\|_{LL}d\tau}}.

Hence we infer from the definition of
\mathbb{V}
introduced in (
4.31
)

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\widetilde{\varrho}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\ln^{+}\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)\Big(\gamma^{\prime}(t)+6\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(t)e^{\mathbb{V}(t)}\Big)\widetilde{\varrho}

(4.37)

\displaystyle+4M(t,x)\sqrt{\widetilde{\varrho}}\;\bar{\varphi}\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{{-\gamma(t)\over 2}-e^{\mathbb{V}(t)}}.

Now, we should choose
\gamma(t)
and
m(t)
such that

\gamma^{\prime}(t)+6\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(t)e^{\mathbb{V}(t)}=0,\quad\gamma(0)=\gamma

and

m(t)=m+2\big(1-e^{\mathbb{V}(t)}\big),\quad\frac{\gamma}{2}\triangleq m-1.

Therefore

\displaystyle\gamma(t)=\gamma+6\big(1-e^{\mathbb{V}(t)}\big)\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\frac{\gamma(t)}{2}+e^{\mathbb{V}(t)}{=}\,m(t)}.

(4.38)

With this choice the condition (
4.31
) follows easily.

Hence, with this choice, we find from (
4.37
)

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+{v}\cdot\nabla\big)\sqrt{\widetilde{\varrho}}\leqslant 2M(t,x)\;\bar{\varphi}\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{-m(t)}.

(4.39)

Remark that
x\mapsto d(x,\Sigma_{t})
is a 1-Lipschitz function. This gives, in particular

\|M(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1.

On the other hand, according to (
4.30
), we know that

m(t)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow\|(\varphi_{t}(\cdot))^{-m(t)}\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leqslant C_{p},\quad\forall p\in[2,\infty].

Using (
4.39
) and the definition of
\widetilde{\varrho}
(
4.34
) yields in view of the above estimate

|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,x)|\leqslant\big(\widetilde{C}_{\infty}+tC_{\infty}\big)\big(\varphi_{t}(x)\big)^{\gamma(t)\over 2},\quad\forall x\notin\Sigma_{t},

(4.40)

where

\widetilde{C}_{\infty}\triangleq\sup_{x\notin\Sigma_{0}}\big(\varphi_{0}(x)\big)^{-m+1}|\nabla\bar{\varphi}_{0}(x)|.

In fact, we can show that (
4.40
) holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This can be done by exploiting the same arguments used in the inviscid case, see in particular (
3.15
).
Hence, it follows that

\gamma(t)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow\|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{\infty}+tC_{\infty}.

Similarly, we obtain for
p\in[2,\infty)

\|(\varphi_{t}(\cdot)\big)^{\frac{-\gamma(t)}{2}}\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{p}+tC_{p},

where

\widetilde{C}_{p}\triangleq\|\varphi_{0}(\cdot)^{-m+1}\nabla\bar{\varphi}_{0}(\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}.

Let us point out that the assumption (
4.10
) together with the fact that
\Sigma_{0}
is compact insure that
\widetilde{C}_{p}<\infty
. This can be proved along the same lines as in (
3.17
).
On the other hand, we have

\gamma(t)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)|=(\varphi_{t}(\cdot)\big)^{\frac{-\gamma(t)}{2}}(\varphi_{t}(\cdot)\big)^{\frac{\gamma(t)}{2}}|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)|\leqslant(\varphi_{t}(\cdot)\big)^{\frac{-\gamma(t)}{2}}|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)|

Consequently, we obtain for any
p\in[2,\infty]

\gamma(t)\geqslant 0\Longrightarrow\|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{p}+tC_{p}.

(4.41)

Remark that the choice (
4.38
) provides the inequality

m(t)\geqslant\frac{\gamma(t)}{2}

which implies that the positivity of
\gamma
will guarantee the positivity of
m
in the same domain. More precisely, by the monotonicity and the continuity of
\gamma
we have

[0,T_{1})\triangleq\big\{t\in[0,T^{\star}):\;\gamma(t)\geqslant 0\big\}\subset\mathtt{I}.

(4.42)

Consequently, we deduce from (
4.30
) and (
4.41
) the existence of a constant
C_{0}>0,
depending only on the initial data, such that for all
p\in[2,\infty]
and
t\in[0,T_{1})

\|\bar{\varphi}(t,\cdot)\|_{W^{1,p}}\leqslant C_{0}(1+t).

(4.43)

It remains to estimate
\psi
, the solution of (
4.28
). We shall restrict ourselves only for
p=2
and
p=\infty
and the other values will be done by an interpolation argument.

\bullet

L^{2}-
estimate
. A standard energy estimate for (
4.28
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\kappa\|\nabla\psi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant

\displaystyle 2\|v(t)\|_{L(\Sigma)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln^{+}(d(x,\Sigma_{t}))\,\psi^{2}(t,x)dx

(4.44)

\displaystyle+\kappa\|\nabla\bar{\varphi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\psi(t)\|_{L^{2}}.

Combining (
4.36
) with (
4.43
) and (
4.44
), it follows that

\frac{d}{dt}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\kappa\|\nabla\psi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant 4A(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln^{+}(d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big))\,\psi^{2}(t,x)dx+\kappa C_{0}^{2}(1+t)^{2},

(4.45)

where
A(t)
is given by (
4.14
). Let
\epsilon\in(0,e^{-1})
, we introduce

\displaystyle f(t,x)\triangleq d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)=f_{0}(\Psi^{-1}(t,x)),\quad f_{0}(x)\triangleq d(x,\Sigma_{0})

(4.46)

and we split

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln^{+}\big(d\big(\Psi^{-1}(t,x),\Sigma_{0}\big)\big)\,\psi^{2}(t,x)
